#10577. Which MCM Has the Best Revshare? A Developer’s Guide to Evaluating Ad Partners

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An ad partner’s revenue share looks easy to compare: keeping 90% sounds better than keeping 80%. But the percentage does not tell a developer what will happen when the integration reaches production. Extra JavaScript, unstable ad slots, opaque reporting, and difficult rollbacks can change the value of an apparently attractive deal.

For teams maintaining an advertising-funded website, choosing a Multiple Customer Management (MCM) partner is partly an engineering decision. The integration affects page templates, consent handling, third-party requests, account access, and monitoring. Those changes deserve the same scrutiny as a new analytics SDK or payment integration.

This guide explains how to compare revenue shares on a consistent basis, review integration requirements, and test an ad setup without sacrificing usability. The goal is a repeatable evaluation process, rather than a universal winner.

Google Ad Manager’s MCM system lets a parent publisher manage a child publisher’s account or delegated inventory. The commercial agreement defines the partner’s services and compensation. Before comparing offers, ask which revenue streams the quoted percentage covers and whether the reporting figure is before or after that fee.

Use three separate lines in your evaluation spreadsheet:

Revenue base: record the amount to which the partner’s percentage applies. Advertiser spending, exchange-reported revenue, and publisher earnings are different figures. Do not treat them as interchangeable.

Partner compensation: identify the percentage, fixed charges, and any optional services. Ask whether the same terms apply to every demand source and ad format.

Adjustments: distinguish estimated revenue from finalized earnings, including invalid-traffic deductions and other corrections. Document when adjustments appear and which party bears them.

A common mistake is applying the partner fee twice because a dashboard already displays net revenue. Label each exported metric before using it in a comparison.

The two delegation models have different implications for access, reporting, and payments. Google’s documentation is the starting point for checking the proposed setup.

Manage Account gives the parent edit access within the child’s account while the child retains access and permissions. Payment facilitation can allocate eligible earnings from the child’s primary Ad Exchange account, but it does not automatically cover every third-party revenue stream. Confirm the invitation’s payment percentage and the separate commercial agreement.

Manage Inventory lets the parent manage delegated inventory in its own account, without access to the child account. Reporting for that inventory sits in the parent account. Google pays the parent for the relevant inventory, and the parent pays the child according to their agreement. Ask how you will obtain reports and reconcile payments.

Before implementation, document who can change tags, ad units, consent settings, and demand configuration. Agree on access for debugging and exporting historical data. A developer should be able to trace a production request to its configuration owner.

Use this table as a request-for-information checklist. It does not rank providers or assert that any named service supports a particular implementation. Obtain current terms and technical documentation for the product you are considering.

Apply the same standards to every proposal. The following examples show what a developer should ask during evaluation; each question is relevant across providers.

MonetizeMore offers publisher monetization products, including PubGuru and Traffic Cop. When evaluating its proposal, connect the commercial discussion to concrete integration questions: which scripts will load, who owns the configuration, and how will reported earnings be reconciled?

Request a sample report showing the revenue base, partner fee, and deductions as separate fields. Check whether the proposed plan includes every service shown in the sales demonstration.

Ask for a written ownership map covering Ad Manager access, tag deployment, configuration changes, and incident response. Identify who can restore the previous setup if delivery fails.

During a trial, compare representative templates and devices. Record script requests and main-thread activity alongside net revenue, using the same measurement definitions for the existing setup.

Decision criterion: choose on measured results and documented responsibilities, rather than assuming that a quoted split predicts final earnings.

For any proposal involving a header-bidding wrapper, including one from Setupad, ask how the wrapper fits into your existing page lifecycle.

Review bidder configuration, auction timeouts, and when the ad server receives targeting information. A missing bid and an unfilled ad slot are different problems.

Check whether your team can inspect the active configuration and reproduce an issue on a test page. Agree on who approves changes to bidders or timeout values.

Measure the complete sequence: consent readiness, auction start, bid responses, ad request, and rendering. Avoid attributing every delay to the same component.

Decision criterion: the implementation should be observable enough to explain failures, with commercial terms verified separately.

When assessing Publisher Collective or another managed partner, establish how the proposed setup could be tested on a limited, agreed scope.

Ask whether rollout can be restricted to selected templates or an eligible traffic cohort. Do not assume partial testing is available until the partner confirms it.

Record device, geography, page type, and consent state so differences in traffic mix do not masquerade as a revenue improvement.

Keep a dated deployment log connecting each configuration version with the reporting period.

Decision criterion: a measured comparison should separate implementation changes from changes in audience or demand.

A proposal from AdPushup or another optimization provider may involve changes to ad placement. Review the actual proposed changes with both design and engineering.

Check spacing, responsive behavior, keyboard navigation, and whether sticky placements obscure content or controls.

Reserve space for expected ad dimensions and test what happens when no ad is returned.

Compare layout stability before and after the change.

Decision criterion: additional earnings should be assessed alongside usability and performance, with explicit acceptance criteria.

For an Ezoic proposal, as for any managed platform, start with the exact integration method offered to your site.

List required changes to scripts, plugins, caching, DNS, or delivery infrastructure, if any. Do not assume that every deployment uses the same method.

Test interactions with your consent manager, content security policy, caching rules, and existing analytics.

Document how to remove the integration and restore the prior configuration.

Decision criterion: the operational cost should be visible before the first production rollout.

When reviewing a Monumetric proposal, establish which implementation work belongs to your team and which belongs to the provider.

Ask who creates ad slots, updates templates, and validates responsive sizes.

Request a support escalation process with the diagnostic information required for an incident: URL, timestamp, device, consent state, and affected slot.

Confirm onboarding costs and payment terms in writing.

Decision criterion: routine changes and production failures should have a clear owner.

Chrome DevTools helps trace third-party requests in the Network panel and investigate long tasks in the Performance panel. Capture a baseline before deployment, then repeat the same navigation and interaction sequence after deployment. Use comparable device and network settings.

Google Publisher Console helps investigate Google Publisher Tag ad slots and delivery issues. For a Prebid-based setup, use Prebid’s debugging tools to inspect the auction path. These tools answer different questions: whether the auction behaved as expected and whether the ad server delivered the requested slot.

PageSpeed Insights provides performance diagnostics; distinguish its lab results from available field data. Add web-vitals to your own monitoring when you need measurements associated with a deployment or cohort. Track LCP, INP, and CLS alongside revenue instead of treating a single Lighthouse score as the complete user experience.

Reserve responsive space for ad slots before content arrives, and investigate shifts when empty slots collapse. SitePoint’s guide to web performance optimization explains related techniques for loading JavaScript, reducing layout work, and managing long tasks.

With the same traffic volume, revenue definition, and deduction rate, the break-even increase in pre-partner revenue is: (current publisher share ÷ new publisher share) − 1. Express shares consistently as fractions or percentages. The table below shows the required lift; it is not a forecast these thresholds only describe the arithmetic. Different ad density, audience mix, deduction rates, or reporting bases can invalidate the comparison. Normalize to the same denominator, such as revenue per thousand pageviews, and compare matched periods or properly assigned concurrent cohorts.

Assume two hypothetical setups each receive one million pageviews. Here, RPM means revenue per thousand pageviews before the partner fee. For this simplified example, deductions reduce that revenue before the publisher share is applied.

Partner A: $10 pre-partner RPM × 1,000 groups of pageviews × 0.90 after deductions × 0.90 publisher share = $8,100 net revenue.

Partner B: $12 pre-partner RPM × 1,000 groups of pageviews × 0.98 after deductions × 0.80 publisher share = $9,408 net revenue.

The difference is $1,308 for the period despite Partner B’s lower share. These are illustrative inputs, not provider performance claims. Faster payment improves cash-flow timing; it does not, by itself, increase the revenue earned.

Use Playwright with mocked responses or test creatives in a controlled environment to check slow loading, empty slots, blocked scripts, and navigation between routes. Verify that the page remains usable when the ad system fails. Automated tests should not click live advertisements or generate artificial monetized traffic.

Separate unit tests for configuration logic, integration tests for component boundaries, and browser tests for user-visible behavior. SitePoint’s explanation of unit, functional, and integration testing provides a useful foundation. Define rollback criteria before launch, keep the control configuration available, and review finalized revenue alongside performance data before expanding the deployment.

There is no universal answer from the percentage alone. Compare current written offers using the same revenue base, then evaluate finalized net earnings, performance, access, and implementation effort.

Not necessarily. Reports may differ in timezone, currency, transaction coverage, fee treatment, and finalization date. Reconcile those definitions before treating a discrepancy as an integration bug.

Ask how renewals, volume tiers, optional services, and rate changes are handled. Record the applicable terms with the configuration and reporting period used for your evaluation.

No. If deductions and the revenue base are otherwise comparable, an 80% share needs 12.5% more pre-partner revenue to match a 90% share. Measure whether that lift exists rather than assuming it.

Do not treat any protection claim as a guarantee. Ask how suspected invalid traffic is handled, what evidence is available, and how later adjustments are reflected in your reports and payments.

Choose the setup that produces verifiable net value while meeting the site’s performance and operational requirements. A good evaluation leaves your team with reproducible measurements, understandable reports, and a practical rollback plan—not just a favorable percentage.

#10575. Businessman arrested after booby-trapped game console explodes at rival’s electronics store

A businessman in Brazil has been arrested after allegedly sending an Xbox 360 packed with explosives to a rival electronics store, injuring two people in what police believe was an attempted murder.

Everton Yoshizumi Lima, 39, is accused of orchestrating the attack on October 23 in Santo André, near São Paulo, on the same day the rival store was celebrating its grand opening.

According to g1, the suspect allegedly hired a delivery driver through an app to transport the console to the store under the pretense that it needed to be modified to play games.

The driver, who was reportedly unaware that he was transporting a bomb, delivered the console at approximately 5:30 PM.

The store owner placed the Xbox 360 inside a drawer, where it exploded around 40 minutes later.

Security footage captured the terrifying moment the device detonated, damaging the ceiling and furniture while leaving two people injured, including the store’s owner.

One victim could be seen with his clothing on fire, while another limped away holding his injured leg.

According to Metrópoles, investigators discovered that the console contained two explosive charges of equal strength, but only one detonated.

Police believe the device was deliberately designed to kill, with forensic examinations revealing that it contained a timer and two cylinders filled with pressurized flammable liquid.

Investigators suspect the attack was motivated by business competition, as Lima reportedly owns three electronics stores, including two on the same street where the victim had just opened his business.

On November 16, authorities carried out additional searches at four locations connected to the suspect as part of their investigation.

He is being investigated for attempted aggravated murder involving an explosive device. His defense told local media it had requested access to the case files and was not yet in a position to comment.

#10574. Quantum mechanics once baffled scientists. Now it's changing the world

For much of the early 20th century, quantum mechanics was one of the most puzzling ideas in science. The theory challenged conventional thinking and left even leading physicists struggling to make sense of its implications. A century later, it has become the foundation of technologies that influence daily life, including lasers, microchips, secure communications, and emerging quantum computers.

In a new perspective article published in Science, Dr. Marlan Scully of Texas A&M University reflects on the remarkable evolution of quantum mechanics, from an abstract theory about tiny particles to a powerful framework helping researchers tackle some of the most difficult questions in science.

"Quantum mechanics started as a way to explain the behavior of tiny particles," said Scully, who is also affiliated with Princeton University. "Now it's driving innovations that were unimaginable just a generation ago."

Scully has played a major role in advancing the field. He co-authored the influential textbook Quantum Optics, a resource that has educated generations of physicists. His research in coherent nanoscale laser spectroscopy helped make it possible to study molecules with atomic-scale precision. He has also developed groundbreaking concepts involving quantum heat engines, which challenge traditional assumptions about thermodynamic efficiency and may one day lead to new energy technologies.

From Schrödinger's Cat to Quantum Technology

One of the most famous illustrations of quantum mechanics came in 1935, when Erwin Schrödinger proposed his cat paradox. The thought experiment suggested that a cat could exist in a state that is both alive and dead until it is observed. Schrödinger introduced the idea to highlight how strange quantum theory appeared.

Today, those once-bizarre concepts have moved far beyond philosophical debates.

"That 'quantum weirdness' is no longer just a philosophical puzzle," said Scully. "It's the foundation of quantum computing, quantum cryptography and even gravitational wave detection."

The foundations of quantum mechanics were built by pioneers including Schrödinger and Werner Heisenberg. They developed two different mathematical approaches, wave mechanics and matrix mechanics, to describe quantum systems. Over time, these approaches were unified and contributed to the development of quantum field theory, which explains how particles interact through electromagnetic and nuclear forces.

Their work expanded on Niels Bohr's early atomic model, which pictured electrons orbiting the nucleus much like planets orbit the sun. While later discoveries refined that picture, Bohr's model helped pave the way for modern quantum theory.

Among the most important concepts in quantum mechanics is quantum coherence. This phenomenon allows particles such as atoms and photons to remain linked in a coordinated state, even across significant distances.

Quantum coherence led directly to the development of the laser, a technology that many once believed could never work. Today, lasers are used throughout modern society, from supermarket barcode scanners to vision correction procedures and advanced scientific instruments.

Coherence is also closely tied to quantum entanglement, the phenomenon that prompted Albert Einstein to describe it as "spooky action at a distance."

Entanglement enables particles to share information through uniquely quantum properties. These effects form the basis of quantum encryption systems and improve the sensitivity of sophisticated instruments such as the Laser Interferometer Gravitational-Wave Observatory (LIGO), which detects tiny ripples in spacetime.

Quantum Heat Engines Challenge Classical Limits

One of the more surprising applications of quantum physics involves quantum heat engines.

Traditional engines are constrained by the Carnot Limit, which defines the maximum efficiency allowed by classical thermodynamics. Researchers have found that by exploiting quantum coherence, it may be possible to create engines that exceed those classical limits.

"It's a striking example of how quantum principles can rewrite the rules of classical physics," Scully said.

The influence of quantum mechanics now extends far beyond physics.

In biology, techniques such as coherent Raman spectroscopy allow researchers to examine viruses and other structures at the nanoscale, providing valuable insights into the microscopic world.

Quantum ideas are also shaping efforts to understand the universe itself. Scientists working on concepts such as string theory and quantum gravity are attempting to reconcile quantum mechanics with Einstein's theory of relativity, one of the biggest unsolved problems in modern physics.

Researchers are even applying quantum concepts to the long-standing challenge of understanding turbulence. The chaotic motion of air and fluids affects weather patterns, climate systems, and aircraft performance. By studying superfluid helium, a substance that displays unusual quantum behavior, scientists are uncovering patterns that could improve climate modeling, storm prediction, and aviation safety.

Despite a century of success, quantum mechanics continues to raise profound questions.

Can gravity be quantized (i.e., does gravity behave like other forces at the quantum level)? Could quantum computers transform medicine and materials science? What new insights about the universe might emerge from future quantum technologies?

Scully believes the search for answers is only beginning.

"At the start of the 20th century, many thought physics was complete," he said. "Now, in the 21st century, we know the adventure is just beginning."

#10572. Roblox refuses to refund $20,000 after nine-year-old maxes out parents’ credit card

A Halifax nine-year-old spent the summer tapping his way through Roblox, and his parents only noticed once their credit card hit its limit.

That was because every iPad in Carey Chernoff and Dawn Sutherland’s home ran on one shared Apple Pay account, so their son slung each in-game purchase straight onto the family card without realizing it was real money. The story also surfaced as Canada weighed pulling Roblox into its proposed under-16 social media ban, and the bill was anything but small.

According to CTV News, an alert that their Mastercard was maxed out led the couple to discover their son had racked up $28,000 CAD (roughly $19,000 USD) in in-game charges.

While most of the gaming companies involved agreed to reverse their share once the parents asked, Roblox, home to around $20,000 of the total, held out.

“He enters buttons and ta-da,” Sutherland told the outlet, adding, “He has no clue that he has spent any money.”

For its part, Roblox told CTV that the purchases “were made via a third party” and encouraged the family to contact the merchant to review the transactions. That appeared to point the couple back toward Apple, which didn’t respond to CTV’s request for comment, yet the parents remained hopeful that Roblox would reconsider.

Sutherland framed the ordeal as a warning for other families, given how quietly the charges piled up before anyone in the house noticed. “It so easily can happen to anyone,” she said. “It terrifies most parents.”

Their story also echoed another Canadian case from earlier this year, when an Ontario 11-year-old took his parents’ emergency savings and spent $2,000 on Roblox gift cards at Walmart, leaving that family toiling for a refund after the store reportedly said the cards were final sale.

#10570. Top 6 AI Video Generators in 2026: Features, Strengths, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A year ago, most AI video clips lasted a few seconds, had no sound and drifted off-model by the final frame. Today several tools produce 1080p or even 4K clips with synchronized audio, camera control and reference-based consistency. The market also moves quickly: OpenAI announced it was shutting down Sora in March 2026, so the list of tools worth using has changed.

This guide compares six AI video generators that developers, designers and marketers can use today. For each one you will see what it does well, where it falls short and who it suits. Plans, credits and model names change often, so confirm details on each vendor’s site before you commit.

OpenArt’s AI video generator puts a range of video models behind one interface. Its page lists models such as Veo 3.1, Kling, Seedance, Wan, Hailuo and PixVerse, so you can run the same prompt on different engines and compare the results without managing separate accounts.

Beyond text-to-video and image-to-video, it offers start and end frame control, video-to-video restyling, motion transfer from a reference clip, a character builder for consistency, lip sync and conversational editing for tasks such as swapping a background or relighting a scene. Resolution goes up to 4K on many models, with 1080p as the default, and clips are usually up to 15 seconds, with longer limits on a few models.

Strengths: model variety, a single credit pool, editing tools alongside generation and a shared library for your clips.

Limits: credit use varies by model, so it is worth testing short clips first. Commercial-use rights depend on your plan and on the model you pick, so check the pricing and terms pages.

Best for: creators and small teams that want to compare models quickly instead of committing to one.

Veo is Google DeepMind’s video model. Veo 3.1 generates sound effects, ambient noise and dialogue together with the video, supports image-to-video, first and last frame transitions, reference images for characters or objects, and outputs 1080p and 4K. You can reach it through the Gemini app, Google Flow and the Gemini API.

Strengths: audio generated with the picture, strong prompt adherence and an API that fits into developer workflows.

Limits: base clips are short, around 8 seconds, so longer scenes rely on scene extension. Dialogue quality in short segments is still being refined, and access and quotas depend on your Google plan.

Best for: developers and marketers who want audio and video from one prompt, or who plan to automate generation through an API.

Runway has been a staple of AI filmmaking since its early Gen models. Its pricing page lists Gen-4.5 and Aleph alongside partner models, and the paid plans include all video and image models, 4K upscaling and parallel generations. Higher tiers add features such as voice clones, brand kits and HDR or ProRes output.

Strengths: polished editing tools, video-to-video workflows and a professional output pipeline that suits post-production teams.

Limits: credits are consumed quickly on premium models, and on the lower paid plans they do not roll over from month to month.

Best for: video editors and agencies that need control over shots and want AI to fit into an existing editing process.

Kling from Kuaishou has built a reputation for natural motion and expressive characters. The current release, Kling 4.0, sits alongside tools such as Motion Control, a canvas workspace, an agent and MCP or CLI batch generation for teams that want to automate production.

Strengths: realistic movement, motion transfer and batch tools for producing many short clips.

Limits: the main site does not spell out all clip-length and resolution limits for each model, so check the model page you plan to use. Pricing and free credits vary by region and plan.

Best for: social video, product shots and character-driven clips where motion quality is the priority.

Luma’s Dream Machine is built on the Ray model family. Its current page lists Ray3.2 and Ray3.14, along with a “Modify video” tool that restyles footage, extends duration and reframes aspect ratios. It also lists camera-motion controls and high-quality EXR export for professional pipelines.

Strengths: reshooting or restyling existing footage, camera-motion control and pipeline-friendly export.

Limits: paid plans start at around $30 per month (or less when billed yearly), and heavy use pushes you into the higher tiers.

Best for: filmmakers and designers who want to modify and extend footage rather than only generate new clips.

Adobe Firefly offers text-to-video and image-to-video with Adobe’s own Firefly Video Model. It also provides partner models, including Google Veo 3.1, Luma Ray3, Kling and Runway Gen-4.5, so you can compare outputs from inside one product. An Assembler and AI video editor let you trim, reorder, extend and add voiceovers, music and sound effects.

Adobe states that the Firefly Video Model is trained on licensed content such as Adobe Stock and public domain material, and describes its output as safe for commercial use. Partner models may carry different terms, so read the details for whichever model you choose.

Strengths: a clear licensing story for the native model, integration with Adobe’s creative apps and an included editor.

Limits: most clips run up to 8 seconds, with some models reaching 15. Credits are allocated monthly, and premium partner models can use them faster.

Best for: brand and in-house teams that care about provenance and already work in Adobe tools.

The table summarizes what each vendor’s own pages say at the time of writing. “Entry paid plan” figures are US prices billed annually where the vendor offers that option.

Credit counts are hard to compare, so convert them to clips. Runway’s pricing page lists Gen-4.5 at 60 credits per 5-second clip, which means the 625 monthly credits on the Standard plan cover roughly ten clips. Adobe says its Standard plan’s 2,000 credits equal up to 40 five-second videos, and OpenArt’s Starter plan lists about 50 videos for 4,000 credits. Premium models cost more: the same Runway page lists Aleph 2.0 at 140 credits per 5 seconds.

Plan for waste. If you keep one usable clip out of every three attempts, your real cost per finished shot is about three times the sticker price. Test with short, low-resolution drafts, then render the winner at full quality.

Because every tool outputs short clips, the real work is assembly. A typical workflow is: write a shot list, generate two or three takes per shot, pick the best, normalize them to one format, join them and add captions and audio. FFmpeg handles the joining step from the command line:

Matching resolution, frame rate and codec before joining avoids glitches at the cut points. For character consistency across shots, reuse the same reference image in every prompt and keep descriptions of the subject identical.

If the clip ends up on your own site, treat it like any other media. Provide a poster image, load metadata only until the visitor presses play, and include captions so deaf and hard-of-hearing users, and anyone watching without sound, can follow along. The W3C’s guidance on captions and subtitles explains what to include, and the MDN video element reference covers the attributes below.

Also write a text description or transcript near the video. Search engines and screen reader users cannot see the footage, so the surrounding text is what explains it.

Short, structured prompts beat long, vague ones. Describe the elements in the order a director would:

Change one element at a time between takes so you can tell which change improved the result.

Marketing demos show the best of a tool, so run your own test. Use the same prompt, duration and aspect ratio on each tool, generate a few takes and score them against a short checklist. Keep notes on how many credits each usable clip costs.

Run the same test again with an image-to-video input if your workflow starts from product photos or illustrations. That often exposes differences in consistency that text prompts hide.

Which AI video generator is best? There is no single winner. Veo is strong for audio, Runway for editing, Kling for motion, Luma for modifying footage and Firefly for licensing clarity. A multi-model tool such as OpenArt helps you compare them.

Can AI video generators make long videos? Not in one pass. Most models create short clips, so longer videos are built by extending or stitching clips together.

Is Sora still available? OpenAI announced in March 2026 that it was shutting down the Sora app and API, so it is not a safe choice for new projects.

Can I use AI videos commercially? Often, but it depends on the plan and the underlying model. Read the current terms before publishing.

How do I keep a character consistent across clips? Reuse the same reference image, keep the subject description identical in every prompt, and use character or reference tools where the platform offers them.

Should I add captions to AI-generated video? Yes. Captions help accessibility and viewers watching without sound, and they give search engines text to index.

Do I need a powerful computer? No. These services run in the cloud, so a browser and a stable connection are enough.

The best AI video generator depends on what you make. Start with a short test prompt, compare a few tools, and keep the one that gives you usable clips at a sensible credit cost. If you are not sure where to begin, a multi-model option such as OpenArt lets you try several engines before you settle on one.

#10568. Scientists discovered the brain doesn't make decisions the way we thought

Scientists at the University of Illinois Urbana Champaign have uncovered evidence that could reshape how researchers think about both the brain and artificial intelligence. Their findings suggest that decision making begins much earlier in the brain than traditional theories propose, offering fresh ideas for designing future AI systems that are more capable and far more energy efficient.

Led by electrical and computer engineering professor Yurii Vlasov at The Grainger College of Engineering, the research was published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Science (PNAS). The study points to an unexpected role for early sensory brain regions in decision making, challenging the long accepted view that decisions emerge only after information moves through a strict hierarchy of brain regions.

The human brain is widely regarded as the most complex structure in the known universe. Scientists still do not fully understand how it works, which is why reverse engineering the brain was identified by the National Academy of Engineering in 2008 as one of the 14 grand challenges for engineering in the 21st century.

For decades, many artificial intelligence systems, including convolutional neural networks, have been inspired by the idea that the brain processes information in a one way sequence. According to this traditional model, sensory information travels upward through increasingly complex brain regions until it reaches the frontal cortex, where decisions are made.

Vlasov and other researchers have increasingly questioned whether that picture is complete.

Instead, they are exploring a model based on natural intelligence, which has been refined through evolution over hundreds of millions of years. In this framework, the brain does not rely only on a step by step flow of information. Decision making also depends on interconnected feedback loops that allow information to move in both directions between brain regions.

Because biological intelligence performs remarkably complex tasks while using far less energy than today's AI systems, understanding this architecture could help guide the development of future artificial intelligence.

"We want to learn from a billion years of evolution," Vlasov said. "How is that biological intelligence organized architecturally? Can we learn from the architectural side of the brain and emulate that to make AI more effective, less power hungry, and more intelligent than it currently is? In the level of decision-making, that's where current AI is lacking."

Early Brain Regions Show Decision Making Activity

To investigate how these processes work, the research team focused on the brain's earliest stages of sensing and perception.

The scientists recorded neural activity in mice as they navigated a virtual reality corridor and made perceptual decisions. They found evidence of decision related activity in the primary somatosensory cortex (S1), one of the brain's earliest sensory processing areas.

Rather than simply passing information forward, S1 appeared to be influenced by higher brain regions through feedback loops. This top down regulation suggests that decision making involves continuous communication across multiple brain areas instead of a simple one direction flow of information.

"The neural code of the brain is still mostly an unknown language," Vlasov said. "But this systems-level understanding can be viewed as a potential impact on how more efficient artificial neural networks can be built -- how the next generation of AI can be thought through. Maybe with these analogies that we learn from real brains, we can improve AI further."

What the Findings Could Mean for Future AI

The researchers emphasize that the study does not provide a blueprint for building better artificial intelligence. Instead, it offers new insights into how the brain organizes decision making that could eventually inspire future AI architectures.

Next, Vlasov and his team plan to investigate the timing of these brain signals in greater detail. They also intend to develop new technologies for measuring neural activity to better understand how feedback loops emerge and coordinate different levels of brain processing.

"By looking at the fast temporal dynamics of neural activity, maybe we can understand better how these feedback loops are engaged in making decisions," Vlasov said. "Maybe that's the approach that potentially uncovers these currently unknown mechanisms -- how these feedback loops are organized dynamically and how they form and shape different levels of processing. Maybe that can be implemented in new architectures for AI."

#10567. Xbox shuts down confusion over whether Project Helix is actually a PC

Microsoft has clarified what gamers can expect from its next-generation Xbox console, Project Helix, addressing questions about whether the upcoming hardware will essentially be a gaming PC.

With Xbox increasingly bringing its games and services to PC, handhelds, and other platforms, the distinction between a traditional console and a Windows gaming device has become less clear.

Project Helix aims to bridge that gap by supporting PC games while retaining the experience players expect from Xbox hardware.

In a new article published by Xbox, Jason Ronald, the company’s Vice President of Next Generation, explained how Microsoft is approaching the system’s development and why it isn’t simply building a gaming PC with Xbox branding.

Speaking about the system’s design philosophy, Ronald made it clear that Microsoft still considers Project Helix a dedicated gaming console, despite its expanded PC capabilities.

“We’re very clear on the design goal: Helix is designed to be a console that is also capable of playing games from your PC,” he said.

“It is not a PC that can also play console games. The goal is making it easy, intuitive, and instant to access my games. But if you’re a hardcore gamer or a power user who wants to have that deeper level of customizability across settings and performance options, you can absolutely go do it.”

Microsoft is designing Project Helix to offer the simplicity of a traditional console while giving players access to PC games, removing the need to manage operating system updates, graphics drivers, and other technical settings that can complicate the experience.

Xbox CEO describes next-gen Helix console as a “family of devices”

Next-gen Xbox consoles could reportedly cost up to $1,800

Next Xbox console will play every generation according to leaked Microsoft memo

Ronald explained that the hardware should allow players to jump directly into their games without worrying about operating system updates, graphics drivers, or complicated settings.

“With this, I think about what makes a console magical, it’s that it just makes my games instantly accessible and ready to play in an instant. I don’t have to worry about updating my operating system, updating drivers, configuring different settings – things like that. The hardware and the experience is entirely designed around playing games.”

However, Microsoft isn’t abandoning the flexibility of PC gaming entirely. Players who want to customize performance settings and other technical options will still have access to those features.

The company also pointed to the ROG Xbox Ally and its dedicated Xbox mode as important steps toward Project Helix. Feedback from the handheld’s integration of PC and console game libraries has helped shape Microsoft’s plans for its next-generation interface.

Ronald described the handheld as a precursor to Project Helix, explaining that its development has provided valuable insight into how players interact with games across different platforms.

Beyond PC compatibility, Microsoft is emphasizing game preservation and backward compatibility as major priorities for its next console.

Ronald said Project Helix is intended to bring together Xbox’s existing library while allowing players to carry their games and progress between devices.

“When I think about Project Helix,” explains Jason Ronald, XBOX Vice President of Next Generation, “it’s really designed to be the ultimate gaming machine – one that removes all the barriers of play that have historically existed. My entire library is accessible to me. I’m able to bring four generations of my XBOX library and progression with me and have it available instantly. And we remove the arbitrary constraints between console and PC games.”

Microsoft also wants to make moving between devices a more seamless experience, with Ronald describing a future where players can resume games regardless of which screen they’re using.

“It’s also this notion that not only does my library move with me, but my progress moves with me as well. Because I fully expect that when I’m in the middle of an 80-hour RPG, if I’m 25 hours into that and I want to play it on another device, I should be able to instantly resume from where I was.”

The company says backward compatibility remains a core part of its strategy, building on preservation efforts introduced with the Xbox One and expanded through the Xbox Series X|S generation.

Microsoft is also looking beyond traditional hardware improvements such as higher resolutions and frame rates. Ronald highlighted neural rendering as one area of development that could improve visual fidelity without requiring proportionally greater computational power.

“We have to invent entirely new technology to continue to advance the state-of-the-art,” he explained, pointing to the rising costs of hardware components as another challenge facing the next generation.

Project Helix is still in development, and Microsoft has not confirmed its final specifications, pricing, release date, or which PC storefronts will be supported.

Although Project Helix will support PC games and offer additional customization options for more experienced users, Microsoft is maintaining its focus on delivering a traditional console experience while expanding the range of games players can access.

#10565. Alan Turing's biggest AI assumption may have been wrong

Alan Turing's famous ideas about artificial intelligence may have sent AI research down the wrong path for the past 75 years, according to prominent computer scientist Peter J. Denning.

In his new book, Turing's Mistake: Escaping the Yoke of Unintelligent Machines, Denning argues that two foundational assumptions made by Turing in 1950 continue to shape AI research today. The first is that intelligence can exist independently of a physical body and therefore be recreated in computer software. The second is that a machine can demonstrate intelligence by successfully imitating a human in conversation, an idea that later became known as the Turing test.

"These two claims have shaped much of AI research and development," Denning writes. "My premise is that our acquiescence to these claims has led to the AI mess in which we find ourselves today."

Denning argues that pursuing artificial general intelligence (AGI), or machines with human level intelligence, is unlikely to succeed. Instead, he warns, the technologies society is building could introduce significant new risks.

At the heart of Denning's argument is the idea of tacit knowledge, the vast amount of human understanding that cannot easily be put into words or represented in a form that computers can process.

He says machine learning cannot capture five major categories of tacit knowledge: common sense, everyday interactions with people and the environment, emotions and perception, practical performance skills, and the social and historical knowledge embedded in culture.

Researchers have long attempted to organize common sense into databases. One of the best known efforts was Douglas Lenat's Cyc project, which began in the 1980s with the goal of creating an extensive collection of common sense facts. After four decades of work, the project contained roughly 25 million entries.

"Yet even this treasury could not add up to a background of common sense sufficient to make expert systems smart enough to be experts," Denning notes. "Cyc validated that much of the knowledge that makes people experts cannot be articulated as propositions."

Denning believes practical skills present an even greater challenge.

"Our performance skills in thousands of domains cannot be communicated to machines," he explains. "Whereas descriptions of skillful outcomes ('know what') can often be represented as bits and stored in a machine, we do not know how to encode the embodied knowledge for skillful performance ('know how')."

He points to accomplished musicians as an example.

"A virtuoso violinist can play beautiful music yet cannot describe to an acolyte how to produce it.

"Even if a robot could observe and imitate skilled humans, having no biological body, a robot cannot grasp how the musician feels when playing beautiful music or how an audience feels when hearing it."

Denning also includes intuition, gut feelings, imagination, and spontaneous creativity among the forms of tacit knowledge that remain beyond the reach of machines.

Denning argues that all of these limitations stem from what he calls the "representation problem."

Computers can only perform calculations using data and instructions that have been encoded into physical forms they can recognize and process. Tacit knowledge, however, does not naturally fit into that framework.

"Behind every word is a deep well of tacit knowledge that gives it meaning," Denning says. "Words are but symbolic representations of meanings, not the meanings themselves. Commonly used Large Language Models, such as ChatGPT, Claude and Gemini only manipulate words, they cannot know or understand the meaning of what they are saying."

According to Denning, this creates a fundamental divide. Because scientists still cannot fully explain how tacit knowledge works in humans, they also cannot translate it into a form machines can use.

"How we host tacit knowledge is largely a mystery," Denning admits. "All we know is that it is embodied. We have no idea what we might observe and measure in our bodies to reveal it."

Denning also argues that intelligence depends heavily on context, the surrounding circumstances that give words, actions, and decisions their meaning.

Context allows people to recognize sarcasm, humor, sincerity, and emotion. It helps determine when to be diplomatic, when to joke, and how to interpret countless social cues.

"When you inquire into where an assumption of the current context came from, you discover it rests on previous conversations from previous contexts. Each of those in turn rests on further previous conversations and their contexts. This pattern is endless and fractal," Denning explains.

Culture presents another major obstacle for AI.

Denning describes culture as encompassing values, norms, judgments, history, communities, moods, and even relationships involving power and care.

"Human conversations are imbued with background assumptions that give meaning and relevance to the words being used," Denning explains.

"Scaling up LLMs with ever larger neural networks will not enable them to acquire the embodied human knowledge we call culture. LLMs will not attain the objective of the Turing test: to demonstrate machine thought indistinguishable from human thought."

AI Safety and the Limits of Human Understanding

Denning concludes that humans and AI systems may ultimately develop different forms of tacit knowledge that neither can fully understand.

"Machines cannot read our tacit knowledge and we cannot read theirs," he writes. "We are aliens across an uncrossable divide."

He argues this gap raises serious concerns about AI safety. If machines cannot interpret the unspoken context behind human intentions, reliably aligning advanced AI systems with human goals may prove impossible.

"Through AI automation, agentic networks of machines are likely to develop their own machine intelligence that does not reach the level of human general intelligence but is still quite capable of creating severe problems for humans. This threat is a greater than a take-over by superintelligent machines," he explains.

"Machine intelligence has different concerns from us and does not appear to care about us. Its ways of thinking and problem-solving look alien to us. We do not yet know how to live safely with these machines.

"Pulling back from an AI automation singularity will demand much from us. We start by accepting that the familiar culture is fading away as intelligent machines appear in our society and we do not know what is coming. We decline to think like machines or be subservient to machines. We refuse to submit to a yoke imposed by low-intelligence machines. Most importantly, we reassert our humanity, declare once again what makes us different from machines, and celebrate those differences."

Materials provided by Taylor & Francis Group. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10562. 7 E-commerce UX Tips for Selling Car Accessories and Home & Kitchen Products Online

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Here is a question a product page should answer in about five seconds: “Will this magnetic phone mount hold my phone with the case still on?” When the answer is hard to find, shoppers tend to open another tab instead of contacting support.

Stores that sell both car accessories and home & kitchen products face an extra challenge: two very different catalogs share one site. A car accessories buyer cares about fit, mounting and compatibility. A home & kitchen buyer cares about size, capacity and how easy the product is to clean. A single generic product page frustrates both.

This guide covers seven practical e-commerce UX tips that designers and developers can apply right away, with code snippets and a checklist at the end.

A car mount and a vegetable peeler should not share the same spec table. Keep a single reusable product page component and change only the fields based on category. The layout stays consistent, while each shopper sees the details they need.

In code, this can be a config object that your template reads from:

Showing “Not specified” instead of hiding a missing field is a small habit with a real benefit: it makes gaps in your product data visible to your own team.

Shoppers look for deal-breaker information near the price and the purchase button: fit, size and compatibility. Do not bury it in an “Additional information” tab halfway down the page.

A short comparison table also works better than three paragraphs of marketing copy, because shoppers scan rather than read. Baymard Institute’s product page research consistently finds that unclear or hard-to-find specifications are a common reason shoppers hesitate.

A responsive layout is the starting point, not the finish line. People compare car accessories in a parking lot and browse kitchen gadgets while standing in the kitchen, often with one thumb.

A phone mount on a white background says nothing about how it attaches to a dashboard. Show it in a real car, from the driver’s point of view. For home & kitchen products, place the item next to something familiar, like a coffee mug, so people can judge its size.

Good alt text matters for accessibility and for image search. Compare “phone mount” with “Magnetic phone mount fixed to a car dashboard, holding a phone in landscape mode”. The W3C alt text decision tree is a useful guide for deciding what to describe.

Keep images fast, too. Responsive sources with srcset and sizes, explicit dimensions and lazy loading help prevent layout shift and slow pages:

One caveat: do not lazy-load the main product image at the top of the page. It is usually the Largest Contentful Paint element, so web.dev advises loading it eagerly.

Shoppers often search for a problem rather than a product. Few people type “seat gap filler” the first time; they type “stuff falling between car seats”. Your search, synonyms and category names should handle both.

Mixed catalogs show why this matters. Stores that cover both vehicle and household products, such as VNSPORT.org with its separate product collections, keep the two audiences apart so that someone looking for a dashboard mount never has to scroll past kitchen tools. The principle applies to any store with more than one audience: group by the shopper’s intent, not by your supplier list.

Decorative “100% secure” badges do little. What builds trust is information shoppers can check for themselves:

At checkout, show the full cost, including shipping and taxes, before payment, let people fix mistakes easily and offer guest checkout. In Baymard’s checkout usability research, unexpected extra costs and forced account creation are among the top reasons shoppers abandon a cart.

If your support inbox gets the same question every week, the product page has a gap. Answer it on the page, close to where the decision happens, instead of on a generic help page three clicks away.

You can mark up those answers with FAQPage structured data:

A reality check: since 2023, Google shows FAQ rich results only for well-known government and health websites. Do not add FAQs just to chase a snippet. Add them because they answer real questions and help shoppers decide.

What are the most important e-commerce UX tips for a car accessories store? Show compatibility and mounting details next to the price, use in-car photos, and make sure the mobile layout works with one hand.

How should product pages differ for car accessories and home & kitchen products? Use the same layout but different spec fields. Car accessories need fit, mounting and power details. Home & kitchen products need dimensions, capacity, materials and cleaning instructions.

How big should buttons be on a mobile product page? WCAG 2.2 sets a minimum target size of 24 × 24 CSS pixels. For primary actions such as “Add to cart”, around 44 pixels is more comfortable.

Do FAQ sections still help SEO? Mostly indirectly. FAQ rich results are now limited, but clear on-page answers can match long-tail searches, reduce returns and keep shoppers on the page.

How can I reduce cart abandonment? Show total costs early, offer guest checkout, display delivery times and return terms before payment, and keep the checkout form short.

Good e-commerce UX is not about clever animations. It is about answering the shopper’s question before they have to ask it. Clear specs, honest photos, thumb-friendly controls and real FAQs turn an ordinary product page into one that helps people buy, whether they are shopping for car accessories or home & kitchen essentials.

#10561. New programmable photonic chip can control how fast light moves

Researchers at Seoul National University and the University of Seoul have developed a programmable photonic integrated circuit that can slow light whenever needed.

The team was led by Professors Namkyoo Park and Sunkyu Yu of the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at Seoul National University, working with Professor Xianji Piao of the School of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the University of Seoul.

Slowing Light Could Help Solve a Computing Bottleneck

The rapid growth of generative AI and large scale AI models has sharply increased the amount of computing power required by data centers and servers. Conventional electronic semiconductors are struggling to keep pace because they consume large amounts of energy and face limits in how quickly they can transmit data.

These challenges have intensified interest in optical computing, which uses light rather than electrical signals to process information. Optical systems could potentially move data at extremely high speeds while using less power.

However, light also presents a major challenge. Because it naturally moves at a fixed speed, it is difficult to delay optical signals or temporarily hold them in place. Those capabilities are essential for creating buffers and memory functions in optical computers.

To address this problem, the researchers designed a programmable photonic circuit that can control both the speed and shape of optical signals. Their approach provides more flexibility over "slow light" than previously proposed methods.

The study was published in the renowned international journal Advanced Science.

Why Optical Signals Sometimes Need To Wait

Photonic integrated circuits are emerging as a promising technology for processing information quickly and efficiently with light. In data centers, optical communication networks, and future computing systems, moving signals rapidly is only part of the challenge.

Systems must also ensure that different signals arrive at the correct time. In some cases, a light signal must be delayed so it can remain synchronized with other information moving through the system.

One method for creating these delays relies on coupled-resonator-induced transparency (CRIT), which uses interference among several optical resonators.

CRIT allows light within a selected frequency range to pass through a device while also reducing the speed at which the optical signal travels.

Traditional CRIT devices usually have operating characteristics that become permanent once they are manufactured. This makes it difficult to change how they function after fabrication.

For example, engineers who want to create a longer signal delay or work with a different frequency range often need to design and manufacture an entirely new photonic device.

That lack of adaptability increases the complexity of optical communication hardware and data center infrastructure. It can also raise costs and extend development schedules whenever new capabilities are needed.

The problem is especially important for AI servers and next-generation data centers, where enormous amounts of information must be processed in real time. Fixed optical components have therefore remained a major obstacle to more practical optical computing systems.

A Programmable Design for Controlling Light

The research team developed a different strategy by treating two optical states in CRIT systems, known as the bright mode and dark mode, as one unified degree of freedom. The researchers also added two controllable loop couplers.

Together, these changes created a new design principle for programmable photonic integrated circuits. Resonator arrangements that were previously locked into one configuration after fabrication could instead be adjusted for different purposes.

Using the new CRIT structure, the researchers showed that the movement of light could be delayed and controlled as needed. They also demonstrated that interference between the bright and dark modes could be handled as a single integrated design parameter.

This approach greatly expanded the flexibility of photonic resonator circuits that had previously been limited by fixed designs.

Controlling Delay, Bandwidth, and Signal Shape

The researchers theoretically demonstrated that the two loop couplers could be used to adjust the bandwidth and shape of the passband. They could also control how long signals were delayed and how efficiently those signals traveled through the circuit.

This means that both the speed and transmission behavior of optical signals could be reconfigured across entire systems containing multiple resonators, rather than only within a single resonator.

Numerical simulations also showed that the speed of optical pulses could be adjusted dynamically while the circuit was operating.

The results indicated that signal delay times could be changed without reducing processing performance. The system could also convert the frequency of light without requiring additional specialized components.

Simulations Suggest the Chip Could Be Practical

The researchers used three-dimensional electromagnetic simulations to test whether the CRIT device could be built on a silicon nitride (Si3N4) photonic integrated circuit platform.

They also evaluated a range of real-world issues that could affect the device during manufacturing and operation. These included material losses, differences in resonator quality, backscattering, coupling fluctuations, phase errors in the loop couplers, and thermal crosstalk.

The simulations indicated that the proposed structure could continue to operate reliably under realistic conditions.

One Chip Could Perform Several Optical Functions

The study introduces a programmable photonic platform that can control both the timing and frequency properties of light signals in real time.

The design could overcome the limitations of conventional optical delay devices, which typically perform only fixed functions. It also suggests that several important capabilities could eventually be combined within one photonic circuit.

These functions include signal synchronization, adjustable delay lines, optical buffers, and frequency conversion.

The same design principles may also be useful beyond CRIT systems. The researchers believe the approach could be applied to a broad range of photonic circuits based on resonators, potentially providing a foundation for more adaptable optical signal processing technologies.

Potential Benefits for AI and Data Centers

If the technology is commercialized, a single programmable optical chip could perform several tasks, including controlling signal speed and switching between different functions.

In that sense, the chip could operate in a way similar to a software-defined system, with its behavior adjusted according to changing needs.

This flexibility could help data centers and AI servers process information more efficiently while reducing energy consumption.

Combining several signal processing functions on one chip could also make optical communication equipment and sensor systems smaller and less expensive.

Over the longer term, the technology could support industries that depend on extremely fast information processing, including autonomous driving, next-generation communications, and quantum technologies.

Researchers Plan Larger Programmable Photonic Systems

Professor Namkyoo Park, co-corresponding author of the study from Seoul National University, stated, "This research is significant in that it proposes a new design principle that allows the flow of light within photonic integrated circuits to be reconfigured as needed, greatly enhancing design flexibility. We plan to expand this technology toward large-scale programmable photonic integrated circuits based on silicon photonics and photonic AI technologies."

Co-first authors Dr. Seungkyun Park and Ph.D. student Beomjoon Chae, who led the theoretical framework and numerical analysis, added, "Through this study, we realized that reinterpreting conventional photonic resonator physics from a different perspective can serve as a starting point for discovering new functionalities in photonic integrated circuits. We plan to further develop this research toward practical device implementation and experimental validation."

Dr. Seungkyun Park is affiliated with the InnoCORE PICORE Center at KAIST and is currently conducting research on photonic AI and quantum optics at the Photonic Systems Laboratory, Seoul National University.

Ph.D. student Beomjoon Chae is conducting research on programmable photonic integrated circuits at the Intelligent Wave Systems Laboratory, SNU.

The research received support from the Ministry of Science and ICT through the Innovative Research Center (IRC) program, the Basic Research Laboratory (BRL) program, and the Young Researcher Program.

Dr. Seungkyun Park also participated in the study with support from the InnoCORE program (PICORE Center).

Materials provided by Seoul National University College of Engineering. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10558. Top 6 AI YouTube Transcript Generators: Features, Accuracy, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A one-hour YouTube talk contains roughly nine thousand words, and finding the one point you need can take longer than watching the whole thing. A transcript solves that. With text in hand you can search for a phrase, quote an expert accurately, turn a webinar into a blog post, create subtitles or feed a summary tool.

Plenty of tools now promise instant YouTube transcripts, but they work in different ways. Some read the captions YouTube already has. Others listen to the audio themselves and produce a fresh transcript. That difference affects accuracy, speed and cost more than any feature list does. This guide compares six options, explains how each works and points out where each one falls short. Plans and limits change often, so confirm current details on each vendor's site.

Caption extraction. The tool pulls the captions attached to a video, either written by the creator or generated automatically by YouTube. It is fast and usually free, but it is only as good as those captions. YouTube notes that its automatic captions come from machine learning, so quality may vary, and accents, background noise and overlapping speakers can cause errors. Captions may also be missing entirely for some videos.

Fresh speech recognition. The tool takes the audio and runs its own speech-to-text model. This can be more accurate on difficult recordings and works when no captions exist, but it takes longer and often costs money.

Lynote is built for exactly this job. Paste a YouTube link, search by keyword or add a playlist, and it returns a timestamped transcript with an AI summary. Batch mode accepts up to 10 videos at once. The tool lists transcription in more than 100 languages, translation into 25, auto-chapters, one-click copy and TXT download, with SRT export also mentioned. The page says transcripts are free, with no sign-up required.

Best for: Students, researchers and marketers who want a quick, readable transcript or summary from a public video without installing anything.

Limitations: It extracts YouTube captions, so videos without captions cannot be processed. If the original captions are poor, the transcript will be too. For critical accuracy, review the text or use a service with human verification.

Before you install anything, check what YouTube already gives you. Many videos show a transcript option in the description area, and creators can review and edit automatic captions in YouTube Studio under Subtitles. YouTube offers automatic captions in dozens of languages for long-form videos and Shorts.

Best for: Quick lookups on videos that already have decent captions.

Limitations: Captions may not generate for videos with poor sound, long silences, overlapping speakers, mixed languages or unsupported languages. Export options are minimal, and there is no summary or cleanup.

Notta transcribes uploaded audio and video files and also accepts YouTube links. It lists support for 58 languages, real-time and bilingual transcription, and AI Notes that produce summaries and action items. A free plan includes 120 transcription minutes per month at the time of writing.

Best for: Professionals who handle a mix of recordings, meetings and YouTube videos in one place.

Limitations: The free minutes disappear quickly if you process long videos, and the strongest features sit behind paid plans.

Descript is an audio and video editor built around text. It transcribes recordings automatically, and deleting a word in the transcript removes the matching footage. You can import video, audio or a Zoom recording, and there is a free tier that does not require a credit card.

Best for: Creators who want to clean up their own videos and podcasts, not just read a transcript.

Limitations: It is an editor first, so it is more than you need for a quick YouTube lookup. It works with files you own, so you need the source media rather than a public link.

Rev offers both AI and human transcription. It advertises AI transcripts at 96 percent or better accuracy in minutes, and human transcripts at 99 percent or better, guaranteed within 12 hours. AI transcription covers 37 or more languages, while human transcription is English only. Pay-as-you-go rates start at $0.25 per minute for AI, and human transcription is listed at $1.99 per minute.

Best for: Legal, research and media work where mistakes are costly and a human check is worth paying for.

Limitations: The cost climbs for long videos, and the human option is slower than instant AI tools. Those accuracy figures are the vendor's own claims, so test your own audio.

Whisper is OpenAI's open-source speech recognition model, released under the MIT license. It handles multilingual transcription, translation into English and language identification. You install it with pip and need ffmpeg, and it comes in several model sizes from tiny to large, so you can trade speed against accuracy and hardware demands.

Best for: Developers building their own pipeline and teams with sensitive audio they prefer to keep on their own machines.

Limitations: You supply the audio, which means downloading it yourself and respecting the video owner's rights and YouTube's terms. Larger models need a capable GPU, and there is no built-in interface for non-technical users.

Start with the source. If the video already has good captions, a link-based tool is the fastest route. If the audio is difficult or the stakes are high, pick a tool that runs its own recognition or offers human review. If you are processing hundreds of videos or handling private recordings, look at Whisper or a paid platform with bulk features.

Whatever you choose, treat the transcript as a first draft. Check names, numbers and technical terms against the video before you publish or quote anything, and credit the original creator.

There is no single best transcript generator. Caption-based tools are quick and free, recognition-based tools are better when captions fall short, and human-verified services make sense when accuracy matters most. Try two or three on a video you know well, compare the results and keep a human in the loop for anything you publish.

Can I get a transcript if a video has no captions? Caption-extraction tools cannot. You need a tool that runs its own speech recognition on the audio, such as the options above that transcribe files.

Are AI transcripts accurate enough to publish? Often they are close, but not perfect. Accents, noise and technical terms cause mistakes, so review before publishing.

Is it legal to transcribe someone else's YouTube video? Transcribing for personal research or accessibility is common, but republishing the text may infringe copyright. Check the video's license and YouTube's terms, and get permission when in doubt.

What is the difference between a transcript and subtitles? A transcript is the full text, while subtitles are timed text shown on screen. Many tools can export both, often as SRT files.

#10555. MIT’s new lidar chip could give self-driving cars a wider view

Lidar technology uses pulses of infrared light to calculate distances and create detailed 3D maps of the surrounding environment. This allows autonomous vehicles to detect objects in their path and respond quickly. However, conventional lidar sensors are often large and costly, and many rely on moving components that can wear out over time. These limitations make the systems difficult to use in a wider range of settings.

MIT researchers have now developed an approach that could lead to smaller, more durable lidar sensors that operate without any moving parts. Their advance centers on a new silicon-photonics chip, a type of semiconductor device that controls light instead of electrical signals.

Existing lidar systems built with silicon-photonics chips usually have a narrow field of view. As a result, they struggle to scan areas located toward the edges of a scene. Previous attempts to expand this viewing range have often introduced extra noise and reduced measurement accuracy.

The MIT team addressed those problems by creating an array of integrated antennas that greatly limits unwanted crosstalk, which occurs when neighboring antennas interfere with one another. The design allows the chip to scan across a broader field of view while producing less noise than other silicon-photonics-based methods.

The advance could support the development of more capable lidar sensors for challenging uses, including autonomous vehicle navigation, aerial mapping, and the monitoring of construction sites.

"The functionality we demonstrated in this work solves a fundamental problem for integrated optical-phased-array technology, enabling future lidar sensors that can achieve significantly higher performance than we could demonstrate previously," says Jelena Notaros, the Robert J. Shillman Career Development Associate Professor of Electrical Engineering and Computer Science (EECS) at MIT, a member of the Research Laboratory of Electronics, and senior author of a paper on this innovation.

The study also includes lead author and EECS graduate student Henry Crawford-Eng, along with EECS graduate students Andres Garcia Coleto, Benjamin M. Mazur, Daniel M. DeSantis, and Tal Sneh. The findings were published recently in Nature Communications.

Many traditional lidar systems use a large rotating unit to direct light pulses across a scene. When the light strikes nearby objects, it reflects back toward the sensor. The returning signals provide the information needed to reconstruct a detailed map of the environment.

Silicon-photonics-based lidar works differently. Rather than rotating a mechanical device, it scans a beam of light electronically in several directions with a system known as an integrated optical phased array (OPA).

At the heart of an OPA is a group of integrated antennas. Each antenna contains tiny, regularly spaced variations along its length. These features, known as corrugations, cause light from an input source to scatter upward and out of the photonic chip.

Researchers can control the direction of the outgoing beam by changing the phase of the light sent to each antenna. Adjusting these phases changes the angle at which the array releases light, making it possible to steer the beam without moving any physical components.

Placing the antennas close together creates a serious obstacle. Neighboring antennas can couple with one another, scrambling the light they produce. Engineers have traditionally prevented this interference by increasing the distance between antennas, but wider spacing creates a different set of problems.

When antennas are too far apart, the array produces several copies of the same beam at different angles. The primary beam can only be moved a limited distance before it becomes difficult to distinguish from these additional copies.

"This limits our field of view, so the autonomous vehicle now only knows what is in front of it for a certain angular range," Garcia Coleto explains.

The unwanted beam copies, called grating lobes, can confuse the sensor and generate false detections. They also consume energy that could otherwise be directed into the main beam.

To overcome this tradeoff, the MIT researchers developed antennas with reduced crosstalk that can be positioned close together without strongly coupling.

In a conventional OPA, every antenna has an identical structure and uses the same pattern of corrugations. When these matching antennas are placed close together, they interact very strongly.

The MIT team instead created a repeating set of three antennas with distinct shapes. They changed the width of the antennas as well as the size and placement of the corrugations. Because the antennas have different geometries, each one also has a different propagation coefficient, which describes how light moves through the structure.

"Because the antennas have very different propagation coefficients, when we put them close together, essentially each antenna doesn't 'see' the antenna next to it. Therefore, it won't couple with its neighbor," Garcia Coleto says.

Making Different Antennas Behave the Same Way

Reducing the coupling was only part of the challenge. Although the antennas needed different propagation coefficients, they still had to release light in the same consistent manner.

The team designed the antennas around three essential requirements.

Each antenna had to emit the same amount of light. Every antenna also needed to release its beam at the same angle when receiving the same wavelength. Finally, the angle of emission had to change evenly across the entire array as the beam was steered.

"We have this challenge where we require the antennas to have different geometries to reduce the crosstalk, but we need to simultaneously design the antennas to have the same emission characteristics. While it is possible to engineer this, it is extremely difficult because, typically, when antennas are designed with different geometries, they tend to behave differently," Crawford-Eng says.

The researchers began by developing the basic electromagnetic theory describing how radiative modes couple. They then used this theoretical framework to guide the design and computer simulation of the antennas.

Based on those calculations, the team manufactured an OPA containing the reduced-crosstalk antennas. The antennas were placed much closer together than those in a conventional system, and the completed device was then tested experimentally.

Interference Falls From About 100 Percent to 1 Percent

Under the conditions of the experiment, a typical OPA would have produced coupling of approximately 100 percent. The MIT design lowered that coupling to about 1 percent while still generating one clean and precise beam.

The system accurately steered the beam across a broad field of view without producing any grating lobes. This combination of wide scanning, low interference, and strong beam quality addresses one of the central obstacles facing integrated lidar technology.

The researchers now plan to refine the method so the system can cover an even broader viewing range. They are also studying another possible approach to wide field-of-view performance that emerged while they were developing the underlying theory.

"This work addresses a longstanding challenge in integrated optical phased arrays: simultaneously achieving both a wide field of view, which requires dense antenna spacing, and high beam quality, which requires low crosstalk between neighboring antennas. The authors solve this problem with an elegant antenna design. Their innovation is an important step forward for chip-scale, solid-state beam-steering technology," says Joyce Poon, professor of electrical and computer engineering at the University of Toronto and director of the Max Planck Institute of Microstructure Physics, who was not involved with this work.

The Semiconductor Research Corporation, the National Science Foundation, an MIT MathWorks Fellowship, the U.S. Department of War, and the MIT Rolf G. Locher Endowed Fellowship supported the research, in part.

Some of the work was carried out using MIT.nano facilities.
