#10525. Self-Hosted LangSmith Alternatives: 4 Tools Tested on One TypeScript Agent

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

LangSmith is closed-source and restricts self-hosting to enterprise licenses, teams that run their own LLMOps stack and want to keep traces on their own servers need an alternative. To find the best fit, we set a baseline by tracing a small TypeScript agent (equipped with two tools) in LangSmith Cloud. Then, we ran the exact same agent through Langfuse, Arize Phoenix, Opik, and AcruxCore using each platform's recommended TypeScript setup. All tests were run on November 5, 2026, and we pulled every trace back through each tool's API to verify the data.

All four alternatives successfully recreated LangSmith’s trace tree for the test prompt: a single agent span containing three LLM calls and two tool calls. The real differences between them come down to how much boilerplate code you have to write, how spans are typed, whether costs are tracked, and the overall server footprint.

Why Teams Look for a LangSmith Alternative

LangSmith remains the best choice if you already use LangChain and allow cloud hosting—it required just 25 lines of code in our test. The alternatives below are for teams that don't fit that profile.

To test these platforms, we built a simple customer support agent equipped with two tools:

Both tools use static fixture data to ensure every test run is identical. The agent runs a standard loop: it prompts the model, executes any requested tools, and stops once the model provides a final answer.

We tested the agent with three specific prompts to see how the tracing handled different levels of complexity:

For our comparison, we focused entirely on the third question because it generates a robust, multi-step trace.

Under the hood, the agent uses the standard openai package to call the qwen3.8-flash model. We originally picked this model based on its reliable tool-calling behavior during early probing thought it once skipped a tool call later.

We used LangSmith as our baseline. By tracing the agent there first, we created a checklist of every field it captured, and we judged all the alternatives against that exact list.

For setup, we followed the recommended TypeScript approach from each tool's documentation. Phoenix didn't have specific instructions for using its register() helper with OpenAI in TypeScript, so we adapted the pattern from their Anthropic guide.

Here is the agent with no tracing. Each tool section adds one tracing file that imports these files and changes nothing in them.

LangSmith traces the plain openai package with two helpers. wrapOpenAI records each model call, and traceable records your own functions. Without traceable on the run and the tools, wrapOpenAI alone gave three separate root runs and no tool runs.

This file adds 25 lines and removes 3, and installs one package.

LangSmith showed one root run with five children in call order: LLM, get_order_status, LLM, get_refund_policy, LLM. Every field it recorded became a row of the checklist table under "Results". Cost appeared only after a custom price entry. In our error run the failing tool was marked as an error, but the root stayed success: traceable turned the throw into a rejected promise that our agent did not await, so the agent carried on and answered.

Langfuse self-hosts as six containers, including ClickHouse, a column store built for large scans. In our measurement the stack held 2.60–2.64 GiB of memory at idle. Its Docker Compose guide recommends at least 4 CPU cores and 16 GiB RAM, with about 100 GiB of disk.

The TypeScript path is built on OpenTelemetry. You start the OTel Node SDK with LangfuseSpanProcessor, wrap the client with observeOpenAI, and wrap your own functions with observe:

This file adds 31 lines and removes 5, and installs four packages. We pinned the OTel Node SDK to 0.223.0, because @langfuse/otel 6.1.1 does not accept 0.300.0.

Langfuse successfully recreated the same trace tree, neatly tagging the spans as AGENT, GENERATION, and TOOL. It captured all the essentials: tool calls, tool definitions, and the complete message history. However, for the LLM output, it only saved the assistant's message—dropping the finish_reason, response ID, and the provider name.

We ran our deployment in Langfuse v4's events_only mode, which turns off the v1 trace endpoint. Because of this, the API didn't return a centralized trace object or root-level sums for tokens and costs, even though the UI calculated and displayed them perfectly (we didn't test other modes).

Costs showed up exactly as expected once we added a custom price entry. Since Langfuse calculates prices based on the specific usage types you define, and we only set rates for input and output, the separate reasoning tokens went unpriced. Finally, during our error test, the observe wrapper let the error bubble up to the agent, correctly flagging both the tool and the root span as an ERROR.

Phoenix self-hosts as two containers, one Python server and Postgres. In our measurement it held 0.47 GiB at idle. Phoenix is source-available under the Elastic License 2.0, so it is not open source. Authentication is off until you turn it on.

The TypeScript path uses Phoenix's register() helper and the OpenInference instrumentation for the OpenAI package. OpenInference is a set of OpenTelemetry attribute names for LLM spans. traceAgent and traceTool wrap the run and the tools:

This file adds 32 lines and removes 3, and installs three packages.

Phoenix rebuilt the same tree, typed as AGENT, LLM and TOOL. Each LLM span keeps the full OpenAI response object, plus the messages as flattened attributes. The root span stores no token count, but Phoenix computes a sum for the root and the trace when you read it. Cost and those sums are only in the GraphQL API and the UI, and the REST API has no per-trace endpoint.

Cost appeared after a custom price entry under Settings > Models, and Phoenix priced the reasoning tokens by falling back to the output price. In the error run, the tool span and the root were marked ERROR, and the tool span carried an exception event with the stack trace.

Opik self-hosts as eight containers, including ClickHouse, MySQL and ZooKeeper. In our measurement it held 3.69 GiB at idle, the most of the four. The self-hosted UI opened without a login, so every trace was readable from any machine that could reach the port.

The TypeScript path wraps the client with trackOpenAI and your own functions with track. By default trackOpenAI starts a new trace for every model call, so the file passes the run's span through the documented parent option:

This file adds 33 lines and removes 5. It installs opik-openai 2.2.142, and npm adds opik 1.11.14 as its peer, because opik-openai asks for opik ^1.8.61.

Opik successfully recreated the trace tree, and its trace object neatly tallies token counts across all LLM spans. In the logs, tool arguments are bundled into an array, and the LLM output only retains the assistant's message.

We did notice a quirk with the documented OpenAI wrapper (tested on the npm package opik 1.11.14): it tags model calls as general rather than llm, which incorrectly leaves the llm_span_count at zero. (We didn't test newer Opik core versions to see if this has changed). Additionally, because our synchronous tools execute in under a millisecond, they log the exact same start time as the subsequent LLM call, causing the UI to list the LLM call first out of order.

Cost tracking is entirely manual. Opik showed no costs by default, and the documentation doesn't mention a custom price table. We checked the configuration tabs on our 2.2.52 instance (Feedback definitions, AI Providers, Workspace preferences) and found no model pricing settings. To see costs, you have to manually assign a price to each individual span yourself—only the spans we hardcoded this way displayed a cost.

Finally, error reporting is highly detailed. If something fails, Opik attaches an error_info block—complete with the error type, message, and full traceback—directly to the tool span, the root span, and the overall trace.

AcruxCore self-hosts as five containers: Postgres, Redis, a Node API and worker, and nginx. In our measurement it held 0.39 GiB at idle, the least of the four. Its quickstart leaves sign-up open and ships a committed default GATEWAY_ENCRYPTION_KEY, which encrypts stored provider keys; replace it. We tested a local build from 2026-09-17.

The TypeScript path AcruxCore's docs put first is its gateway. The official openai client keeps working: you point its baseURL at ACRUXCORE_BASE_URL + "/gateway" and call a model registered in AcruxCore, which forwards each call to an OpenAI-compatible provider. The gateway records every model call as an llm span. The SDK's step helpers add the rest: traces.step opens the agent span, traces.wrap wraps each tool, and two headers file the gateway's spans under the agent span:

This file adds 30 lines and removes 5, and installs one package. It builds its own client, so it does not use provider.ts. The gateway refused our team API key, so the client uses a gateway virtual key created in the dashboard.

On this path every model call passes through the AcruxCore server, which none of the other four tools need. If you do not want a proxy in the request path, the SDK's register() helper sends OpenTelemetry spans from your process instead. On that path the step helpers do not join the openai package's spans, so you write the agent and tool spans by hand.

AcruxCore rebuilt the same tree, typed as agent, llm and tool, in call order. Each LLM span keeps the full response, the provider and the cached-token count, and the trace carries a token total. The LLM spans do not keep the tool definitions sent to the model, and no reasoning-token count was recorded. The trace took its name from the question, not from the agent span, and each LLM span is named by its start time.

Cost appeared on every LLM span and as a sum on the trace, from the custom price we set on the model in AcruxCore's model list. In the error run, the step helpers marked the tool span and the agent root as error with the error message, with no status code of ours. The API returned no stack trace.

All five tools accept traces over OTLP, the OpenTelemetry wire format. trace-otel.ts sends the same spans with no vendor package: the OTel Node SDK, the OTLP/HTTP protobuf exporter and the OpenInference OpenAI instrumentation. The exporter reads its endpoint and headers from environment variables:

We ran the two-tool question once per backend and changed only environment variables: the endpoint, the headers, and for Phoenix a resource attribute that sets the project name (without it, spans go to a default project). "Accepted" means the exporter reported success, which it does only for an HTTP 2xx, and we read the spans back through each API.

Opik stored the root and tool spans as general although AGENT and TOOL kinds were sent; the cause is Not tested. LangSmith mapped AGENT to its chain type. If you instrument with plain OTel and OpenInference attributes, your raw spans are not tied to one vendor, but span types, cost and trace views still differ by backend.

The first table covers setup: how much server each tool needs, how much code we changed in the agent, and how cost and the plain OTel exporter worked. Phoenix and AcruxCore had the smallest servers, and AcruxCore needed the fewest lines among the self-hosted tools. Opik was the only one without a price table.

The second table checks each trace against what LangSmith recorded for the same run, one field at a time. All five rebuilt the same tree with tokens and durations. They differ in span types, how much of the LLM output they keep, where token sums live, and how much detail they store for the failing tool.

Token totals for the two-tool question ranged from 1,910 to 2,111. Each tool traced a separate run and reasoning length varies, so these numbers do not compare the tools.

All five tools successfully traced our test agent, so the right choice really comes down to your team's specific constraints, infrastructure preferences, and how tracing fits into the rest of your MLOps tooling.

All four alternatives successfully recreated LangSmith’s trace tree. The real deciding factors are boilerplate code, span typing, cost tracking, and server bloat.

LangSmith remains the simplest option for LangChain users who can utilize the cloud. Otherwise, your choice depends on your priorities: Langfuse for community size, Phoenix for a lightweight Postgres footprint, Opik for strict Apache-2.0 compliance with deep error logging, or AcruxCore for combined tracing and tool governance on minimal hardware. Best of all, if you stick to standard OpenTelemetry spans, you aren't locked in—you can try them all and easily pivot later.

#10523. Scientists just made quantum computer operations 1,000 times faster

Quantum computers remain highly vulnerable to errors and tiny disturbances from their surroundings. The longer a quantum operation takes to complete, the more time there is for those errors to build up. Researchers at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden have now developed a method that can perform a broad range of advanced quantum operations more than a thousand times faster. The advance tackles a major obstacle in the field and could help move quantum computing closer to becoming fault-tolerant.

Quantum computers could eventually transform areas such as drug discovery, energy technology, cryptography, artificial intelligence, and logistics. Before that can happen, however, these machines need to become much more dependable.

A major challenge is that quantum computations can be disrupted by extremely small environmental effects. Electrical noise, cosmic radiation, and overheating can all introduce errors while information is being processed.

Traditional computers can experience errors too, but decades of development have produced reliable error correction methods that can quickly detect and repair them. Quantum systems are much harder to protect because the information they use is extraordinarily delicate.

"The fundamental building blocks of quantum computers, known as qubits, are so sensitive that even the smallest disturbance can cause the quantum state to deviate from the target, resulting in the loss of information. If too many errors accumulate before they can be corrected, the computation can fail," says Lei Du, researcher in Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden, and lead author of the theoretical study published in the journal Physical Review Letters.

A Different Way to Protect Quantum Information

To make quantum computing more resilient and eventually fault-tolerant, researchers are investigating new ways to shield quantum information from errors. One promising strategy uses so-called bosonic quantum codes*.

Instead of assigning quantum information to individual qubits, this approach stores it in microwave fields inside superconducting circuits.

"Rather than storing quantum information in individual qubits, bosonic codes encode information in the microwave fields found within superconducting circuits. This approach has been shown to provide stronger protection against certain types of errors," explains Tangyou Huang, researcher in Quantum Technology at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Quantum Operations More Than 1,000 Times Faster

Working with bosonic quantum codes is not simple. Creating and controlling the required quantum states has traditionally involved guiding a quantum system through thousands of repeated driving cycles.

That process can take considerable time, and every additional cycle creates another opportunity for outside disturbances to interfere with the calculation. In quantum computing, speed is therefore closely tied to reliability.

Chalmers researchers Lei Du and Tangyou Huang have now proposed a different strategy. Rather than constructing the desired quantum states one small piece at a time, their method can perform a wide variety of operations much faster.

"Our method shows that a diverse range of quantum operations on bosonic states can be completed within a single driving cycle, rather than the several thousand cycles that have been required previously. This makes the operations both faster and more efficient, while reducing the risk that disturbances will corrupt the information before the process is finished. It represents an important step towards fault-tolerant quantum computers," says Lei Du.

The new approach is built around Quantum lattice gates, a recently proposed universal set of quantum gates developed by the same research team.

These gates act somewhat like shortcuts. Instead of requiring a long sequence of repeated control steps, they can allow the intended quantum operation to be completed in just one driving cycle. That could make the process faster, simpler, and less vulnerable to errors.

"You can think of it like building a large Lego castle. Instead of assembling it brick by brick and risking mistakes along the way, quantum lattice gates act like pre-built Lego modules that can be connected quickly and efficiently," says Tangyou Huang.

Designed for Superconducting Quantum Computers

The technique is especially well suited to superconducting quantum computers, which are among the most prominent technologies being developed in the international push toward large-scale quantum computing.

Chalmers University of Technology is also using superconducting technology as it develops a 100-qubit quantum computer.

"A key advantage of our approach is that it can be implemented using existing superconducting quantum circuit platforms. We are already discussing possible experimental realizations with colleagues at Chalmers, and we hope to see a demonstration of the method in the near future," says Tangyou Huang.

The researchers say the work addresses a central problem facing the field: efficiently producing and controlling quantum states that are capable of helping correct errors.

"Our results address one of the major bottlenecks in the field: how to quickly and reliably create and control the error-correcting quantum states that could play an important role in future quantum computers," says Lei Du.

More About Bosonic Codes, Quantum Lattice Gates and Floquet Control

*Bosonic quantum codes store quantum information in the states of, for example, microwave or optical resonators rather than in individual qubits. They are considered a promising tool for quantum error correction because they can provide built-in protection against certain types of errors.

Quantum operations are needed to process that stored information. Quantum lattice gates are a recently proposed collection of basic building blocks that can be used to control bosonic quantum states. By combining and designing these gates, researchers can carry out a wide range of more complicated quantum operations.

One method for implementing these operations is known as Floquet control, which uses periodic control signals to drive a quantum system. Earlier Floquet-based techniques have generally depended on slower processes involving many repeated driving cycles. The approach developed at Chalmers can instead perform quantum lattice gates directly in a single driving cycle, making some operations more than a thousand times faster.

The scientific paper "Single-Period Floquet Control of Bosonic Codes with Quantum Lattice Gates" has been published in Physical Review Letters. The authors are Tangyou Huang, Lei Du, and Lingzhen Guo. The researchers are affiliated with Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden and Tianjin University in China.

The research was funded by the National Natural Science Foundation of China (NSFC), the Wallenberg Centre for Quantum Technology (WACQT), and the Knut and Alice Wallenberg Foundation.

Materials provided by Chalmers University of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10520. Tiny nanolaser could cut computer energy use in half

The development of a nanolaser could mark an early step toward a future in which microchips communicate entirely with particles of light.

Researchers at DTU have created a tiny nanolaser that could eventually help make computers, smartphones, and data centers faster while using much less energy. The breakthrough was published in the scientific journalScience Advances.

In the future, thousands of these lasers could potentially be integrated onto a single microchip. Instead of moving information around a chip with electrical signals, devices could transmit data using photons, the particles that make up light.

"The nanolaser opens up the possibility of creating a new generation of components that combine high performance with minimal size. This could be in information technology, for example, where ultra-small and energy-efficient lasers can reduce energy consumption in computers, or in the development of sensors for the healthcare sector, where the nanolaser's extreme light concentration can deliver high-resolution images and ultrasensitive biosensors," says DTU professor Jesper Mørk.

Mørk co-authored the study with, among others, Drs. Meng Xiong and Yi Yu from DTU Electro.

Much of the internet already relies on light to carry information through fiber optic cables. Inside computers, however, data still moves through electronic circuits using electricity. That approach produces heat and can limit how quickly information can be transferred.

Nanolasers could bring optical communication directly onto microchips. By generating light signals efficiently inside the chip, they could allow information to move with very little energy loss, potentially making future devices faster, cooler, and more energy efficient.

Mørk estimates that using nanolasers in computers could reduce energy consumption by as much as half.

The compact DTU nanolaser is an important step toward that goal. Future chips designed around light-based communication would likely require thousands of extremely small, efficient lasers working together to transmit information across the chip.

The new nanolaser was created in DTU's clean room facility, DTU Nanolab. According to Mørk, the device pushes beyond the conventional limit for how small a laser can be made.

At its core is a structure known as a nanocavity, which traps and concentrates light within an exceptionally tiny space. Until now, achieving such intense confinement at this scale had been considered extremely difficult.

When researchers shine a beam of light onto the device, both photons and electrons become concentrated in the same microscopic region. This interaction allows the laser to function at room temperature while requiring unusually little energy.

The light-trapping structure used in the nanolaser was originally developed by Professor Ole Sigmund's group at DTU Construct.

The next major challenge is making the nanolaser operate using electrical power. If researchers can achieve that, the technology could have broad applications across computing, communications, and healthcare.

Computers and smartphones could potentially deliver greater performance while consuming less electricity. Data centers, which require enormous amounts of power, could also see substantial reductions in energy use, potentially leading to significant climate benefits.

In healthcare technology, the nanolaser's ability to concentrate light into an extremely small area could support ultra-sensitive sensors and high-resolution imaging systems.

Researchers estimate that the remaining technical challenges could be solved within the next 5-10 years.

Materials provided by Technical University of Denmark. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10517. AI uncovers hidden Ozempic side effects across 400,000 Reddit posts

Artificial intelligence is giving researchers a new way to listen to what patients are saying about popular GLP-1 drugs. After analyzing more than 400,000 Reddit posts, a University of Pennsylvania team identified several symptoms reported by people using semaglutide (Ozempic, Wegovy, and Rybelsus) and tirzepatide (Mounjaro and Zepbound) that may not be fully represented in clinical trials or official regulatory information.

The study, published recently in Nature Health, examined more than five years of posts from nearly 70,000 Reddit users. Two categories stood out as particularly deserving of further investigation: reproductive symptoms, including changes in menstrual cycles, and problems involving body temperature, such as chills and hot flashes.

The findings do not establish that the medications caused these symptoms. Instead, researchers say the massive collection of spontaneous patient reports may reveal signals worth examining more closely.

"Some of the side effects we found, like nausea, are well known, and that shows that the method is picking up a real signal," says Sharath Chandra Guntuku, Research Associate Professor in Computer and Information Science (CIS) at Penn Engineering and the study's senior author. "The underreported symptoms are leads that came from patients themselves, unprompted, and clinicians could potentially pay attention to them."

What Patients Report Outside Clinical Trials

Clinical trials are designed to determine whether treatments work and to identify important safety problems, but they cannot necessarily capture every symptom that matters to patients once a medication is being used by a much larger population.

"Clinical trials generally identify the most dangerous side effects of drugs," adds Lyle Ungar, Professor in CIS and a co-author on the study. "But they can fail to find what symptoms patients are most concerned about; even though social media is not necessarily representative, a large collection of posts may reflect additional concerns."

The distinction is important. The study found associations in what people discussed online, not proof that GLP-1 drugs were responsible for those experiences.

"We can't say that GLP-1s are actually causing these symptoms," notes Neil Sehgal, the study's first author and a doctoral student in CIS advised by Guntuku and Ungar. "But nearly 4% of the Reddit users in our sample reported menstrual irregularities, which would be even higher in a female-only sample. We think that's a signal worth investigating."

Using Social Media as an Early Health Signal

The idea of mining online conversations for clues about drug safety predates today's AI boom. In 2011, Ungar participated in one of the earliest efforts to use material created by internet users to identify possible adverse effects from medications.

Social media can capture experiences that patients discuss with one another but may never formally report to a doctor, drug manufacturer, or regulator.

"Online patient communities work a lot like a neighborhood grapevine," says Ungar. "People who are living with these medications are swapping notes with each other in real time, sharing experiences that rarely make it into a doctor's office visit or an official report."

Since then, online patient communities have expanded enormously. That has made social media a potentially valuable source for studying how medications affect people in everyday life, although gaining access to platform data has become more difficult.

Traditional clinical research remains essential, the researchers emphasize, but online conversations can provide information far more quickly when millions of people begin using a drug.

"Clinical trials are the gold standard, but by design, they are slow," says Guntuku. "This is not a replacement for trials, but it can move much faster, and that speed matters when a drug goes from niche to mainstream almost overnight."

AI Makes Massive Social Media Analysis Possible

One of the biggest obstacles has always been scale.

Guntuku describes the approach as "computational social listening," which uses computational methods to identify patterns in large collections of online conversations about health.

Patients, however, rarely describe symptoms using standardized medical terminology. One person might describe feeling unusually cold, another might mention constant chills, while a clinician could categorize those experiences using a specific medical term.

Researchers therefore need a way to translate everyday language into standardized categories. One important reference is the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA), which provides terminology widely used to classify medical conditions, symptoms, and adverse events.

Previously, connecting huge numbers of informal social media posts with standardized medical terminology required enormous amounts of work, limiting how much data researchers could realistically analyze.

Large language models such as GPT and Gemini are changing that equation by allowing researchers to process and categorize vast amounts of text more consistently and quickly.

"Large language models have made it possible to do this kind of analysis much faster with a level of standardization that could be difficult to achieve before," says Sehgal.

Unexpected Symptoms Emerge From 400,000 Posts

The researchers stress that Reddit users do not represent the overall population of people taking GLP-1 drugs. Reddit users tend to be younger, are more likely to be male, and are disproportionately located in the United States.

Even with that limitation, the analysis produced a reassuring sign that the approach was detecting genuine patterns. Many of the symptoms discussed by Reddit users closely matched already known effects of semaglutide and tirzepatide.

About 44% of users included in the study described at least one side effect. Gastrointestinal problems were the most common, consistent with the nausea and other digestive issues already associated with these medications.

More intriguing were symptoms that appeared frequently enough to attract the researchers' attention but may not be as well represented in current drug labels or conventional adverse event reports.

Nearly 4% of users who reported side effects described reproductive symptoms. These included changes in menstruation such as bleeding between periods, heavy bleeding, and irregular menstrual cycles.

Users also described changes involving body temperature, including chills, feeling unusually cold, hot flashes, and symptoms resembling a fever.

Fatigue was another notable finding. It was the second most frequently reported complaint in the Reddit data, even though relatively few clinical trials reported fatigue often enough for it to reach established reporting thresholds.

Why Menstrual and Temperature Changes Are Interesting

One possible reason these reports caught researchers' attention involves the hypothalamus, a small but extremely important region of the brain. Among its many jobs, the hypothalamus helps regulate hunger, hormones, reproduction and body temperature.

"These drugs are thought to work by engaging part of the brain called the hypothalamus, which helps regulate a wide variety of hormones," says Jena Shaw Tronieri, Senior Research Investigator at Penn's Center for Weight and Eating Disorders and a co-author of the study. "That doesn't mean the medications are necessarily causing these symptoms, but it could suggest that reports of menstrual changes and body temperature fluctuations are worth studying more systematically."

The researchers are not proposing that this biological connection proves GLP-1 drugs are responsible. Instead, it offers another reason to test the patient-reported patterns more carefully through controlled research.

Turning Online Conversations Into Research Leads

For now, the team hopes the results encourage scientists and clinicians to pay closer attention to symptoms that patients repeatedly discuss online.

"They're clearly on patients' minds, and that's worth paying attention to," says Sehgal.

The researchers also want to broaden their analysis beyond Reddit and beyond English-language communities. Doing so could help determine whether the same patterns emerge among different groups of people and on different social media platforms.

"We don't really know yet whether what we're seeing on Reddit reflects the experience of GLP-1 users globally, or whether it's particular to the kind of person who posts on Reddit in the United States," Ungar says.

In the longer term, rapid AI analysis of online patient conversations could potentially become an early detection system for emerging health concerns involving drugs, supplements and wellness products.

That could be particularly valuable for substances that become popular online faster than conventional research can keep up. Loosely regulated or unregulated products, including injectable peptides, can spread quickly through communities on Reddit, TikTok and other platforms. Discussions among users may therefore provide some of the earliest indications of unexpected effects.

"The whole point of this kind of approach is that it can move quickly, and that's exactly when it's most valuable," says Guntuku.

This study was conducted at the University of Pennsylvania School of Engineering and Applied Science. The authors report no outside funding. Tronieri reports receiving an investigator-initiated grant, on behalf of the University of Pennsylvania, from Novo Nordisk and receiving consulting fees from Currax Pharmaceuticals, LLC. The other authors report no conflicts of interest.

#10515. GTA 6 running at 30 FPS “makes sense” experts say after Rockstar’s confirmation

Digital Foundry says that Rockstar Games would be “asking for trouble” in trying to run GTA 6 at anything other than 30 FPS after the feature was confirmed.

In just a matter of hours, Grand Theft Auto fans will finally get to boot up GTA 6 and experience Rockstar Games’ long-awaited game. As the day draws nearer, though, there has been plenty of attention on how the game looks.

As part of their preload trailer, Rockstar confirmed that GTA 6 will run at 30 FPS. This has been the case for their games at launch, but it still disappointed some fans who wanted a boost to 60 FPS or 40 FPS after such a long wait for the game.

It was also a talking point for Digital Foundry during episode 290 of their DF Direct Weekly, as Richard Leadbetter explained why he believes Rockstar couldn’t scale things up.

“There’s no comments on whether QSSR will be on PlayStation 5, so we’re just going to have to wait and see what happens there,” he said, noting that the “world was crying out” for the PS5 Pro features.

“You’ve got to consider the frame time budgets. At 30 FPS, they’ve got 33.3 milliseconds to process the game world, the simulation, all of that stuff and thats a fair amount of time but they’ve decided from the design perspective that they need it.

“Now, 60 FPS, you’ve got half that. You’ve got to do the same job in half the amount of time. It’s not viable. The obvious thing is to accept that and say, what about 40 FPS? It’s only 10 FPS more than 30, but that’s not the way it works.”

Leadbetter noted that even at 40 FPS there would be a “huge amount of optimization or cutbacks” to hit the 25 millisecond frame time.

#10512. How to Build an Accessible Customer Reviews Section with HTML and CSS

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Customer reviews are often displayed on business websites, but presenting them effectively requires more than adding a few star icons and customer quotes. Developers need to consider semantic HTML, responsive layouts, accessibility, and how review content fits into the overall user experience.

A well-built review section makes customer feedback easy to read without distracting visitors from the rest of the page. It should also work across different screen sizes and remain understandable to people using assistive technologies.

Let's look at how to build one using standard HTML and CSS.

The first step is to structure review content properly. Instead of placing everything inside generic

elements, use meaningful HTML elements to describe the content.

For example, a review can use

as its container and
for the customer's actual feedback.

This structure separates the review from its attribution and gives the section a clear heading. The rating also includes an accessible label so screen-reader users can understand what the stars represent.

For real websites, developers should display genuine customer feedback and avoid presenting invented testimonials as actual customer experiences.

A review section might look attractive on a desktop but become difficult to read on smaller screens. CSS Grid provides a straightforward way to create a flexible layout without relying on JavaScript.

The auto-fit and minmax() combination allows cards to adjust to the available space. Instead of defining a fixed number of columns for every device, the browser fits as many cards as the container can comfortably accommodate.

Developers working with more complex interfaces can explore SitePoint's guide to responsive web design for additional layout techniques.

A responsive layout should remain readable when viewed on a phone. Avoid fixed card widths, excessive padding, and text sizes that force users to zoom.

You can also adjust spacing at smaller viewport widths:

Test the section at several viewport sizes rather than relying on one desktop preview. Check that long customer names wrap correctly, cards do not overflow, and text remains comfortable to read.

Some review components use animated sliders, automatic scrolling, or hover effects. These features can create problems for visitors who prefer reduced motion or navigate with a keyboard.

A simple approach is to avoid unnecessary animation. If transitions are included, respect the visitor's system preference:

The MDN guide to using media queries for accessibility explains how developers can adapt interfaces to user preferences.

Also make sure interactive controls have visible keyboard focus styles. A carousel should provide usable previous and next controls, clear labels, and a way to pause automatic movement if it is used.

On a production website, reviews may come from a database, a content management system, or an external review service. Keeping the content separate from the presentation makes the component easier to maintain.

Businesses can also use an online reputation management platform to monitor customer feedback and understand how their brand is perceived across online channels.

For example, a developer could store each review with fields such as:

The frontend can then render the same component for every review. This avoids manually creating separate HTML for each customer and makes future design changes easier.

Developers should also validate incoming data, handle missing fields, and avoid displaying private customer information without permission.

Before publishing, check the component in different browsers and screen sizes. Test keyboard navigation, screen-reader labels, text wrapping, and color contrast. If reviews are loaded dynamically, check how the section behaves when there are no reviews or when the data request fails.

A useful review component does not need complicated animations or a large JavaScript library. Semantic HTML, flexible CSS Grid layouts, accessible labels, and reliable content handling provide a strong foundation.

Customer feedback can only be useful on a website when visitors can understand and navigate it easily. By treating reviews as a proper interface component rather than decorative content, developers can create sections that are maintainable, accessible, and consistent with the rest of the website.

#10511. The shape behind the Einstein problem just revealed strange new physics

A mathematical shape that drew global attention for solving a decades-old puzzle is now revealing an unexpected connection to physics. Researchers have found that structures based on the shape can make light form unusual chiral patterns, pointing to new ways of exploring how geometry can influence optical behavior.

In a study published in Nature Communications, researchers from the Institute of Industrial Science, The University of Tokyo, and collaborating institutions built optical structures inspired by the "Smith hat." This unusual shape is known for solving the so-called Einstein problem in mathematics. When the team illuminated the structures with laser light, they observed diffraction effects unlike those seen in conventional quasicrystals.

The Shape That Solved the Einstein Problem

The Einstein problem asks whether a single tile shape, known as a "monotile," can cover an entire surface without creating a repeating pattern.

Familiar tilings such as checkerboards and honeycombs repeat in a regular way. An aperiodic monotile, by contrast, can fill a surface without ever settling into a repeating arrangement.

In 2023, researchers discovered the first such monotile, the Smith hat. The finding attracted widespread attention because it provided a long-sought solution to the mathematical problem.

"What is especially fascinating about the hat tile is that, although the resulting pattern appears irregular at first glance, it is actually constructed from the honeycomb lattice," says lead author Yuto Moritake. "We wanted to see whether this unique shape could also produce any unexpected physical phenomena."

Turning a Mathematical Pattern Into an Optical Structure

To test that possibility, the researchers created nanoscale versions of the pattern on silicon nitride films using electron beam lithography.

When laser light was directed at the structures, the resulting diffraction patterns formed distinctive pinwheel-like shapes. These patterns directly revealed the chiral character of the aperiodic structure.

Chirality refers to a form of handedness in which a structure and its mirror image cannot be perfectly matched. In this case, the unusual arrangement of the monotile pattern caused the light itself to display a chiral response.

"We found that the diffraction patterns themselves become chiral because the structure lacks mirror symmetry," explains senior author Masaya Notomi. "This kind of optical response is fundamentally different from that observed in conventional quasicrystalline materials."

Light Responds to Direction and Polarization

The researchers also found that the diffraction pattern changed depending on both the direction and polarization of the incoming light.

When the physical structures were mirrored, their optical behavior reversed as well. This showed that the response of the light was directly tied to the symmetry of the underlying pattern, revealing a new type of symmetry-controlled optical behavior.

"These results open a new direction of research on the fusion of quasiperiodic order and chirality," remarks Moritake. "Monotile patterns provide a platform for exploring optical phenomena that emerge from the interplay of symmetry, chirality, and aperiodicity."

From Abstract Mathematics to New Optical Physics

The researchers say structures inspired by monotiles could eventually contribute to technologies designed to manipulate light, control polarization, and support advanced optical devices.

More broadly, the findings show how a discovery that began as an abstract mathematical puzzle can lead to unexpected physical effects. The Smith hat was first celebrated for solving a question about how shapes can cover a surface. Now, its unusual geometry may also help researchers uncover new ways to control and study light.

#10508. MIT’s tiny flying robot gets 450% faster with AI

Tiny flying robots may one day help rescuers search for people trapped beneath collapsed buildings after earthquakes. Because of their small size, these robotic insects could potentially move through narrow spaces that larger drones cannot enter while avoiding walls, debris, and falling objects.

Until recently, however, aerial microrobots have been far less nimble than the insects that inspired them. They typically moved slowly and followed relatively simple flight paths.

Researchers at MIT have now demonstrated a new approach that gives an insect-scale flying robot much greater speed and agility. The team developed an AI-based controller that allows the robotic bug to perform demanding aerial maneuvers, including repeated body flips.

Using a two-part control system designed to balance performance with computational efficiency, the researchers increased the robot's speed by about 450 percent and its acceleration by about 250 percent compared with their previous best results.

The robot was agile enough to complete 10 consecutive somersaults in 11 seconds, even while wind disturbances tried to knock it away from its intended path.

"We want to be able to use these robots in scenarios that more traditional quadcopter robots would have trouble flying into, but that insects could navigate. Now, with our bioinspired control framework, the flight performance of our robot is comparable to insects in terms of speed, acceleration, and the pitching angle. This is quite an exciting step toward that future goal," says Kevin Chen, an associate professor in the Department of Electrical Engineering and Computer Science (EECS), head of the Soft and Micro Robotics Laboratory within the Research Laboratory of Electronics (RLE), and co-senior author of a paper on the robot.

Chen is joined on the paper by co-lead authors Yi-Hsuan Hsiao, an EECS MIT graduate student; Andrea Tagliabue PhD '24; and Owen Matteson, a graduate student in the Department of Aeronautics and Astronautics (AeroAstro); as well as EECS graduate student Suhan Kim; Tong Zhao MEng '23; and co-senior author Jonathan P. How, the Ford Professor of Engineering in the Department of Aeronautics and Astronautics and a principal investigator in the Laboratory for Information and Decision Systems (LIDS). The research was published in Science Advances.

AI Gives the Robot a Smarter Flight Controller

Chen's group has spent more than five years developing robotic insects.

The team recently created a more durable version of their tiny robot, which is about the size of a microcassette and weighs less than a paperclip. This newer design has larger flapping wings that support more agile flight. The wings are driven by soft artificial muscles that contract rapidly enough to produce extremely fast wingbeats.

The physical design had improved, but the robot's controller remained a major limitation. This controller acts as the robot's "brain," determining where the robot is and deciding how it should move. In earlier versions, a human had to tune the controller by hand.

For the robot to fly with the speed and aggressiveness of a real insect, the researchers needed a system that could handle uncertainty while rapidly solving complex control problems.

A controller powerful enough to do that would normally demand too much computation to operate in real time, especially because the aerodynamics of such a lightweight flying machine are highly complex.

To solve this problem, Chen's group collaborated with How's team to develop a two-step AI-driven control system. The design combines the robustness needed for difficult, high-speed maneuvers with enough computational efficiency to work in real time.

"The hardware advances pushed the controller so there was more we could do on the software side, but at the same time, as the controller developed, there was more they could do with the hardware. As Kevin's team demonstrates new capabilities, we demonstrate that we can utilize them," How says.

Teaching a Tiny Robot to Plan Difficult Maneuvers

The first part of the system uses what is known as a model-predictive controller. This type of controller relies on a dynamic mathematical model to predict how the robot will behave and determine the best sequence of actions for safely following a desired flight path.

Although this method requires substantial computing power, it can plan difficult movements such as aerial flips, sharp turns, and aggressive changes in body angle. The planner also takes into account limits on the amount of force and torque the robot can produce, helping it avoid movements that could lead to a crash.

Repeated flips are particularly challenging. To perform one somersault after another, the robot must slow down in exactly the right way so that it begins each new flip under the proper conditions.

"If small errors creep in, and you try to repeat that flip 10 times with those small errors, the robot will just crash. We need to have robust flight control," How says.

The researchers then used this expert planner to train a "policy" built on a deep-learning model through a process called imitation learning. The policy serves as the robot's real-time decision-making system, determining how and where it should fly.

In effect, imitation learning allows the team to capture the behavior of the more computationally demanding controller in a faster AI model that can operate quickly enough for real-time flight.

A major part of the challenge was generating enough useful training data to teach the policy how to handle aggressive maneuvers without creating an unnecessarily large data set.

"The robust training method is the secret sauce of this technique," How explains.

During flight, the AI-based policy receives information about the robot's position and converts it into real-time commands controlling factors such as thrust force and torques.

Experiments showed that the two-step control system dramatically improved the robot's performance. The insect-scale machine flew 447 percent faster and achieved a 255 percent increase in acceleration.

It also completed 10 somersaults in only 11 seconds while remaining within about 4 or 5 centimeters of its planned flight path.

"This work demonstrates that soft and microrobots, traditionally limited in speed, can now leverage advanced control algorithms to achieve agility approaching that of natural insects and larger robots, opening up new opportunities for multimodal locomotion," says Hsiao.

The team also demonstrated a movement known as a saccade. In insects, this involves pitching the body sharply, rapidly moving to a new position, and then pitching in the opposite direction to stop. This quick acceleration and braking can help insects determine their position and maintain clear vision.

"This bio-mimicking flight behavior could help us in the future when we start putting cameras and sensors on board the robot," Chen says.

One major goal for future research is to equip the microrobots with onboard cameras and sensors. That could eventually allow them to fly outdoors without depending on a sophisticated external motion capture system.

The team also plans to investigate whether onboard sensing could help groups of the robots avoid crashing into one another and coordinate their movements while navigating.

"For the micro-robotics community, I hope this paper signals a paradigm shift by showing that we can develop a new control architecture that is high-performing and efficient at the same time," says Chen.

This research is funded, in part, by the National Science Foundation (NSF), the Office of Naval Research, Air Force Office of Scientific Research, MathWorks, and the Zakhartchenko Fellowship.

#10504. Quantum computing’s “dark horse” just proved it can go universal

A practical quantum computer must eventually be able to handle any type of quantum algorithm, much like a conventional laptop can run many different kinds of software. Researchers have now demonstrated a new way to reach that level of flexibility using unusual quantum objects known as non-Abelian anyons.

Scientists from the University of Chicago Pritzker School of Molecular Engineering (UChicago PME), Harvard, Stony Brook University, and Quantinuum created and tested a full set of operations based on non-Abelian anyons. Their results provide the first experimental demonstration that this approach can support the broad range of operations required for universal quantum computing.

"We demonstrated a so-called universal gate set -- meaning that if you store information in these emergent versions of quarks, and you move them around, you can do any quantum computation you might want to do," said Ruben Verresen, assistant professor of molecular engineering at UChicago PME and a co-author of the new study published in Nature.

A Possible Shortcut Around Costly Quantum Error Correction

The strategy could do more than help create a general-purpose quantum computer. It may also offer a more efficient route toward reliable quantum machines.

Quantum computers are extremely vulnerable to errors, so researchers typically protect information by distributing it across many physical qubits. These error correction methods can preserve data, but they usually do not provide every operation needed to perform universal quantum computation on that protected information.

To fill the gap, engineers often rely on specially prepared resources called "magic states." Producing them usually requires an intensive purification process known as distillation, which can consume a large fraction of a quantum computer's available qubits. The new results suggest that non-Abelian anyons may offer a way around that expensive step.

"Non-Abelian codes are a dark horse in the race to quantum error correction," said Henrik Dreyer, managing director and scientific lead at Quantinuum's Munich office and a co-author of the study. "In this work we show the first universal gate set in a non-Abelian code, which demonstrates that fault-tolerant computations can in principle be done without resorting to magic state distillation or cultivation, which are the most expensive operations in standard quantum error correction codes."

Ordinary qubits encode information using two basic states, along with quantum combinations of those states. Non-Abelian anyons work in a fundamentally different way.

These anyons do not appear as ordinary standalone particles in nature. Instead, scientists create them inside quantum circuits by entangling many conventional qubits into a collective state that behaves as though it were a new type of particle with its own unusual rules.

"The way I think about these codes is they're creating little universes -- alternative universes, but ones that reflect some of the properties of our own," Verresen said.

Each non-Abelian anyon carries an internal state that changes when one anyon is moved around another in a process known as braiding. The sequence of those braiding operations matters (that's what non-Abelian means), allowing information to be encoded and manipulated in ways that are unavailable to ordinary particles.

Because the information is spread across many entangled qubits rather than stored in a single location, it can also be naturally protected from some of the small disturbances that frequently disrupt conventional qubits. Braiding the anyons can also perform computational operations.

In 2024, a research team that included Verresen used a Quantinuum trapped-ion computer to create anyons associated with a symmetry group called D4 -- the rotations and reflections that leave a square unchanged -- demonstrating this form of non-Abelian order on quantum hardware for the first time.

That experiment showed that the unusual particles could be created and manipulated, but braiding them alone was not sufficient to carry out every operation required for universal quantum computing.

"In that work, we didn't demonstrate that those emergent forces were enough to do quantum computation," said Verresen. "That particular universe we created was not powerful enough."

Fusion Unlocks Universal Quantum Operations

For the new study, the researchers switched to another symmetry known as S3 -- the rotations and mirror-image flips that leave an equilateral triangle unchanged -- and created the corresponding anyons on Quantinuum's H2 trapped-ion processor using 54 entangled qubits.

The S3 system had the properties needed for universal quantum computation, but only when braiding was combined with another operation called fusion. During fusion, two anyons are brought together and the resulting state is measured.

The underlying concept was proposed theoretically in 2003 by Carlos Mochon, then a student of John Preskill at Caltech. Turning that theoretical idea into an experiment that could actually run on quantum hardware, however, required extensive additional theoretical and experimental work.

The researchers used pairs of anyons to encode "topological qutrits" which store three possible levels of quantum information instead of the two levels used by ordinary qubits.

By combining different braiding and fusion operations, the team demonstrated three key tools: one entangling gate produced through braiding and two different measurements created through fusion. Together, those operations can in principle produce any quantum operation, including operations that braiding alone could not achieve.

Beyond their possible computing applications, these unusual quantum states could also help researchers investigate fundamental aspects of physics.

"It is gratifying to see ideas we have spent our PhD work thinking about realized in the lab, and it has been made possible by remarkable advances in quantum hardware over the past few years," said Anasuya Lyons and Chiu Fan Bowen Lo, graduate students at Harvard University in the group of Ashvin Vishwanath who helped lead the work.

The researchers also demonstrated that non-Abelian anyons could directly produce a magic state using topological operations -- sidestepping the costly distillation process used in most quantum systems.

The experiment did not yet include active error correction. Instead, the researchers focused on testing the individual building blocks of the method and confirming that they could create a magic state consistent with theoretical expectations.

"So far, we've ignored the question of error correction. Here, it's more like a proof of principle," Verresen said.

The next major step will be combining these operations with active error correction. If that can be achieved, non-Abelian anyons could eventually become a practical basis for large-scale, fault-tolerant quantum computers. Verresen is already working with other PME researchers on new techniques for stabilizing non-Abelian quantum memories.

Materials provided by University of Chicago. Original written by Sarah C.P. Williams. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10503. School offers students $85 prize if they don’t skip for GTA 6

A Catholic school in England is offering students the chance to win a £65 Amazon voucher if they attend classes on GTA 6’s launch day instead of staying home to play Rockstar’s highly anticipated game.

With Grand Theft Auto 6 finally launching on Thursday, November 19, fans around the world have been booking time off work to explore Vice City.

However, St John’s Catholic School & Sixth Form College in Bishop Auckland, England, is taking a different approach, offering students a financial incentive to show up for class.

In a Facebook post titled “WANTED: ONE MORE DAY OF PATIENCE,” the school urged students to treat attending classes as their final side quest before GTA 6 arrives.

“We know many of our students will be counting down the days until the highly anticipated release of the GTA video game on Thursday,” the school wrote.

“But before you start your next mission, there’s one objective left to complete: Attend school on Thursday!”

The school warned that skipping classes to play GTA 6, or staying up all night gaming and being too tired to attend, would both count as a “mission fail.”

Instead, it announced a special one-day attendance challenge, with every student who attends and completes the school day entered into a draw for a £65 (around $85 USD) Amazon voucher.

“That’s the equivalent value of a copy of GTA… just for turning up to school and completing your Thursday mission,” the school explained.

“No cheats. No shortcuts. No skipping cut-scenes. Just one day of attendance for a chance to win.”

The promotion comes as schools and workplaces prepare for the impact of GTA 6’s release, although not everyone is trying to stop fans from taking time off.

“Your controller will still be there when the bell goes. The game will still be downloadable after school,” it said.

The school also made clear that students who have no interest in GTA 6 can still enter the prize draw, provided they turn up and complete the school day.

#10502. Despite promising a heady mix of Vampire Survivors and Warhammer fan-service, Warhammer Survivors is somehow less than the sum of its parts

You know my favourite thing about Vampire Survivors? The slow build of strength, the swelling power fantasy, the edging. Developer Poncle has proved itself incredibly adept at goading players into ever more ludicrous setups, demonstrating that with some patience, a canny eye for build-crafting, and some very rudimentary knowledge of your surroundings, even an old man armed with garlic can be an extinction level event for the amassing undead. Warhammer Survivors sadly never reaches those heights.

Created by Warhammer 40,000: Boltgun 2 studio Auroch Digital, Warhammer Survivors is - as its name suggests - an officially-licensed attempt to transplant developer Poncle's winning Vampire Survivors' formula to the Warhammer universe. Cue 40+ characters ripped from Warhammer lore getting the action-roguelike treatment, crossbows instead of clock lancets, rocket launchers instead of fire wands, bomb Squids instead of skeletons… you get the idea.

But this fusion doesn't really work. While Vampire Survivors was always a bit of an uphill battle from the start, it still hooked you with a thread that pulled you forward, tempting you with power-ups and other intriguing toys to play with en route. If hour one was a slog, hour five sparked curiosity, and hour 10 was a romp. In Warhammer Survivors, however, hour 10 feels like just as much of a slog as hour one. And even though Warhammer Survivors offers two alternate universes - the grimdark future of 40K and the old-school fantasy of Age of Sigmar, each a full game in its own right - this does little to alleviate the grind.

It's all so slow. Characters move slowly, upgrades come slowly, unlocking the mode that makes things move a bit faster comes slowly. Every inch feels like a mile, and the moreish power creep - tantalisingly propulsive in Vampire Survivors - is out of reach for so long per run here, it's discouraging. That's not to say there isn't any resolution to all the tension. Every now and then, you'll roll the right dice, appease the right gods, make the right moves. Your miniature pixel heroes will overcome the odds, and burn through the writhing mass of Orks, Skaven, Tyranids, Nurgle, or whatever other bastards are trying to get you down today.

The Survivors genre - sometimes known as 'bullet heaven' now - lives and dies on the strength of the over-powered nonsense that emerges as all your weapons and evolutions start to harmonise with each other in brutal synchronicity. Warhammer Survivors seems reluctant to let you get to this level, though. Even 30 hours in (!), some runs will end before they ever really get started - no matter how many skips, rerolls, or other chance-altering upgrades you've committed to your save file.

And because this is really two games glued into one, you have to do all the requisite grinding and levelling on both sides. With massive maps and impossibly slow character movement (especially on some of the bulkier characters), collecting all the items per location is more of a chore than it is fun. This wouldn't be so bad if these items were just curios, but in most cases they're pretty significant quality-of-life enhancers. Why are you hiding them from me, Auroch!?

It's almost impressive how Warhammer Survivors manages to feel like a step back from Poncle's original in many ways: yes, there's more content, but there appears to be a lack of understanding of why this genre immediately took off and spawned a myriad of imitators. The very worst weapons are the only ones available at the beginning, so unlocking new characters and armaments is necessary rather than a fun bonus. Lore is paper thin - in Vampire Survivors, we had a blank canvas and the lack of lore didn't really matter, but Warhammer is steeped in its own mythos, so relegating it to merely set dressing feels remiss. Some of the synergies between items and their evolutions don't really make sense. The maps are awkward and fussy to navigate. The locked requirements to take on stage-end bosses are even more cryptic than in Vampire Survivors. In almost all the ways that matter when it comes to fun, Warhammer Survivors pales in comparison to its forebear. This wouldn't be so egregious if the game wasn't trying to emulate it quite so completely, but it is - and as such, all the actually decent design choices hidden behind layers of frustration are marred.

As a Warhammer and Survivors fan, I've found myself floating back to the game, even if - sometimes - my eyes roll back into my head within minutes of my first run. But it's not a complete disaster: on those occasions where the stars do align, Warhammer Survivors finds its way to a power fantasy worthy of both franchises. You'll grab a Flamer early doors, then pick up the passive Glue to reduce incoming damage. You'll gain the Heavy Flamer, the Boltgun, and the projectile-multiplying Dice Cube, eventually unlocking the Heavy Bolter - slicing through enemies and all but melting the face off that pesky Prophet of Gork.

I just wish there wasn't so much friction in actually getting to that point. I imagine Warhammer Survivors is the sort of game I'll keep on my harddrive (and on multiple devices; it costs a fiver and it's on Game Pass) for a while. If you can endure the teething pains and engage with the wobblier systems, there's the usual Survivors payoff waiting for you as the screen fills with pixelated blood and countless helpless enemies fall to your blades. Maybe the stress of the early game even makes this catharsis even better. So even with its qualms, Warhammer Survivors is still an enjoyable, lo-fi stress reliever to snack on in between other, meatier games.

#10500. Build a Hardware Diagnostic Log with Vanilla JavaScript

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A device disconnects halfway through a test. The application reports a timeout, the firmware restarts, and the next attempt succeeds. Where should you start?

For developers working with connected devices, browser interfaces, or hardware prototypes, an application error is often only the final symptom. Replacing code without recording the conditions around a failure can leave you chasing the wrong layer.

This tutorial builds a small browser-based diagnostic log. It records measurements, preserves the test conditions, and exports evidence for another developer to review. You need basic HTML and JavaScript knowledge; no framework or connected instrument is required.

1. Define the question before collecting data Consider a hypothetical development board that disconnects when a peripheral starts. Several explanations remain possible: a firmware exception, a transport problem, or a power supply issue. Start with a testable question: “Does the measured supply voltage change when this peripheral starts?” That question tells you what to record and what to keep constant.

Use a known-good board of the same revision where available. Match the firmware, cable, supply, peripheral, and workload. A comparison loses value when several conditions change together.

Record the observation separately from the explanation:

The hypothesis remains provisional. A reading taken after the device restarts does not establish what happened during the failure.

2. Choose evidence that answers the question Application logs, firmware traces, and bench measurements answer different questions. A timeout establishes that an operation did not finish in time; it does not identify a failed component.

For a view of specialist equipment used in phone-board troubleshooting, DIYFIXTOOL’s overview of Chip-Level Diagnostic Tools discusses thermal imaging, BGA X-ray inspection, and inductance testing. Treat it as a vendor-authored introduction to equipment categories; use instrument documentation and board-specific references when interpreting measurements.

The log below deliberately leaves interpretation to the operator. Enter a numeric value, its unit, and the conditions under which it was measured. Do not turn an isolated reading into an automatic “faulty chip” verdict.

For physical measurements, follow the board and instrument documentation and work within your training. This tutorial covers the software record rather than a procedure for probing or repairing a powered device. 3. Build a small, accessible form

Save the following as index.html and serve it through your usual local development server. For example, if Python is installed, run python -m http. server 8000 in its directory, then open http://localhost:8000. 4. Preserve data without hiding failures

The application stores an array of records as JSON. On reload, it parses that JSON and checks the expected field types before rendering it. Parsing alone does not validate the structure of stored data.

SitePoint’s guide to looping through JSON responses provides useful background on working with objects and arrays after parsing JSON. Each table cell uses textContent, so a note containing HTML is displayed as text rather than inserted as markup. The MDN documentation for textContent explains this behavior.

Storage operations sit inside try...catch blocks because browser persistence can fail. The interface keeps new measurements in memory and offers an export when saving fails. It does not claim those measurements will survive a reload. For another practical vanilla JavaScript project, SitePoint’s **Markdown previewer tutorial **explores browser UI construction and persistence.

As **MDN’s localStorage documentation **explains, storage belongs to the document’s origin. Opening the application on a different port may therefore show a different log. This prototype is a single-browser tool, so export important sessions and avoid recording customer identifiers or private device contents.

5. Compare measurements under matching conditions Suppose two hypothetical readings are 3.29 V and 3.08 V. Their difference is −0.21 V. That arithmetic is straightforward; its meaning depends on the rail specification, measurement uncertainty, workload, and timing.

Do not add a universal voltage threshold to the application. A device-specific limit should come from its specification or an established test procedure.

If the failure is brief, a manually entered steady-state measurement may miss it entirely. Record that limitation. The next investigation may require a time-correlated capture rather than another static reading.

When sharing a session, include the firmware revision, board revision, instrument settings, and reproduction steps. A colleague should be able to repeat the comparison without guessing how the reading was obtained. 6. Verify the workflow before relying on it Try adding a decimal reading and a negative reading, then reload the page. Confirm that both values and their units remain intact. Enter a note containing test and check that it appears literally in the table.

Export the JSON and inspect the record count. Then simulate blocked storage and confirm that the interface reports the failure while allowing an export of the current session.

For a larger application, add editable entries, instrument metadata, and explicit baseline records. A shared service would also need access controls, server-side validation, and a policy for retention. These additions should follow the needs of the diagnostic workflow.

Turn a symptom into a reproducible investigation

The finished prototype captures a useful minimum: what was measured, where, when, and under which conditions. Its value comes from preserving evidence that another person can inspect.

When an application error crosses into firmware or hardware, that record helps you choose the next experiment. Repeat the failure, change one variable, and document what the new evidence supports before deciding what to fix.

#10499. World of Warcraft: Forever's magical opening week reminds us all about the power of the MMO, and the threat it poses to your free time

World of Warcraft: Forever is a time vampire. Since its release last Wednesday, I have spent 78 hours in Azeroth. Some of that I was AFK, sure. But for many of those hours, I was lost, happily, in the rolling hills and vast planes of Blizzard's MMO - now enhanced, beautified, and given a new social energy that makes the old feel wonderfully new.

The original World of Warcraft is so beloved, so influential, that I wasn't convinced Forever could build upon it without cheapening the overall experience; without creating glaring clashes between its new additions and legacy elements that, in some instances, are now over two decades old. And while there's still plenty I haven't seen after 78 hours and 35 levels, I can speak to one truth: WoW Forever is stunning work.

Perhaps it's best to start with WoW: Forever's most obvious changes: its new lighting and weather effects. People love to speak about getting lost in MMOs, being consumed by their digital worlds, but as much as I've enjoyed World of Warcraft in the past, its ageing graphics tech never managed to ensnare me that way. Forever's new visual elements, though, have gone a long way toward pulling me in.

As I look out to sea, waves roll across the ocean. Inland, water flows fast along the rivers of Stranglethorn Vale. These are largely cosmetic additions, sure, but they make Azeroth feel so much more real. Dun Morogh's snowstorms - likely the first exposure many players will have to Forever's new weather effects - transform a peaceful, picturesque landscape into something hostile at a moment's notice. It's the same for Tanris; its vast desert now blighted with sandstorms that bring raging life to an otherwise fairly barren zone.

World of Warcraft; Forever's enhancements go beyond visual, of course. Before launch, Blizzard described Forever as an opportunity to address imbalances, and to show a bit of love to underserved playstyles and classes. That's why I decided I'd play as a druid and work toward creating a balance caster: a ranged DPS druid far slower to level than a feral one, and far less useful as a magic DPS compared to a mage. In other words, an unoptimal choice for the truly silly.

I actually levelled a balance druid to max in Classic World of Warcraft some years back and it was an arduous process. In Forever, though, adjustments made to the spells and talents have made it much less frustrating so far. You do more damage, for starters, and alterations to the talent tree and unlockable skills mean you don't run into the sort of mana problems that crippled balance druids previously. From what I've seen, all the ugly duckling class specialisations - like Retribution Paladins and Enhancement Shamans - have received similar changes.

My interest had been similarly piqued before launch by the talk of WoW: Forever's new dungeons. I still need more time to understand how these additions affect the wider leveling process, but already I can see they've been crafted with care, fitting nicely in the gaps between the original dungeons on a progression level.

Hall of Thanes is a Ragefire Chasm equivalent for Alliance players, serving as a solid introduction to dungeons for newcomers before they head off to The Deadmines and beyond. Here, you delve into the bowels of Ironforge, navigating tombs and tunnels in search of treasure and tackling foes beneath the seat of dwarvish power. It's simple and clean like all early game Classic dungeons are, made with such faithfulness to the original vision that I could be convinced it was always present.

It's also a lovely narrative introduction to one of the world's many antagonists, the Dark Iron Dwarves. In the original World of Warcraft, players would first encounter them across a scattering of quests before being thrust towards Blackrock Mountain. Now, though, they show up early and immediately plant intrigue. What's the deal with these drilling machines? What about the fire elementals they're with?

Forever's new dungeons also introduce some well-crafted quests. Anyone who's played Classic WoW can recount the frustration of dungeons that, thanks to some awkward design, often require two separate completions to finish off. Blackfathom Deeps is a prime example; there are never enough twilight pendants to go around, forcing players to go back in for a second run if they want to tick it off. As such, many, myself included, don't bother. World of Warcraft: Forever, though - with some exceptions - appears to favour a one-and-done approach to dungeons. In the new Excavation Site: Wetlands, for instance, there are just enough raptors hidden in tall grass for everyone to complete their hides in one go. A little thing, but meaningful.

But beyond all this, perhaps the most wonderful thing about World of Warcraft: Forever is how full it feels. It's lively, bustling with action and excitement. Some of this is just that new MMO energy, but Blizzard's new mega server approach, which vastly increases the number of players you'll see doing quests compared to a regular server, is undoubtedly a significant factor too.

Every major city is packed with people having their own adventure, each quest zone is littered with heroes doing their bit to save the world. And this opens the door to social interactions, the beating heart of any MMO. The sight of dozens of players jumping off a zeppelin or flooding onto an elevator in unison is a joy, and grouping with strangers for an escort quest or an elite quest is a curative for digital hermits.

At one point, I find myself drawn into a conversation with a mage during a quest for troll ears. The mobs were hotly contested, and Horde players were quick to snatch tags. The few minutes I spent there, talking about what's going on and how we're finding the game were lovely, and World of Warcraft: Forever's set-up encourages so much more of this kind of thing.

So, while I probably have plenty more sleepless nights left to go before I max out my druid, World of Warcraft: Forever has made a strong early impression. It's taken the original game and expanded on it in genuinely meaningful ways, leaving the things that worked untouched while enriching the elements needed some love. It's a wonderful point to jump into the game if you're a new player, then, and a refreshed classic full of twists for the returning veteran. Whatever the coming months and years may bring, this opening week has been magical.
